WOMEN'S CHAT.

MA DGF: they are entertaining in their way. Cricket is played with wickets and a bowler, the defending side being armed with small nets to prevent the batsman from i scoring by placing the ball n goals arranged round the table.- Golf is rather less like the real thing, the "ball" consisting of a small disc or counter, which is struck by a small ivory club. Bunkers, water jumps, and other diflicultiesare provided round the table, but though this seems to be the more popular game, it does not allow of so many players taking part in it as cricket. Donning Women's Attire.—When the master of a house dresses in woman's clothes, and does his own housework, we are presented with a picture which no one would look for except on the stage. Yet this was the aoknowledge.d practice of a malecorrespondent who recently wrote to the papers, on the vexed question of corsets. He worecorsets, he said, at all times, but his reason for adopting full feminine dress when at horns was that he could clean doorsteps and windows, and do general housework, without attracting notice. One can quite understand that such a disguise might pass very well with strangers, but it would scarcely deceive neighbours, or the regular tradespeople. In any case it is not a solution of the servant problem that is likely to be generally adopted. Jewellery and Colours.—The choice of colattr, when deciding upon a gown, should of a certainty be governed by the complexion and eyes of the wearer, but more often it is the jewellery which a woman posses-Ses that decides this important matter. The owner of some fine torquoises, knows, that they look their best with green, the woman who owns rubies is aware that they are even more lovely with palest pink, palest blue, or light grey. It is also well known that amethysts are charming with pale yellow, and that -pearls with cream velvet, is an exquisite scheme. Jewellers are again finding rubies and diamonds to be the favourite gems, and [possibly this is owing to the fact that the ruby is the name stone of December—and that it is supposed to bring luck when given in the right month. The Craze for White.—White ball and dinner gowns are almost a livery, and for young girls, white satin— the bodice of course being fluffy and pretty—is very popular. Though a little more costly to start with, these gowns are much more economical in the long run, than chiffon, tulle, or net frocks, many of which barely last out one dance. The satin, if good, can be cleaned two or three times, and after that, is always meful as a slip. Dance dresses are almost all built short, that is, they sweep the floor only, and possess no " tails," but the long transparent sleeves that hang from the shoulders like wings, are almost as much a nuisance as the long trains used to be. Many girls wind them round the arms, when dancing, but the effeFt in such instances is much more funny than graceful. December Weddings.—lf the old adage "Happy is the bride that the sun shines on" is true, it seems a little curious that so many brides should tempt fate in this rash fashion by being led to the altar in all the bravery of their wedding finery in the month of December. Yet the fact remains that numbers of women do this, as may he seen day by day from the columns of the daily papers. The number of Society weddings that have taken place this month is almost unprecedented,and moreover two brides have appeared at the altar in black. This was, it is true, in one instance relieved with white, and in the other with helitrope.but the gowns, delicate lace, silk mousseline, and crepe de chine, were of sable hue. The "going away " costumes have, for the most part, been white, and trimmed with fur these look very charming for mid-winter bridal travelling gowns. s The Habit of Bickering.—Lady Campbell recently offered the suggestion that parents should discourage the habit of bickering whieh prevails in ninny families. The advice is wise and a great contribution would be niade to the domestic peace of a large number of people, if families had the good sense to adopt it. The people who are continually snapping at, or contradict- ing the other members of the family,may say and think that they do not metnetny harm, but they would find life in the home a great deal happier if they would discontinue a practice which is fatal to that cordial affection and tranquilitYl\ without which a home is scarcely worthy of the narne. At the same time, they would get rid of the habit which impresses visitors most unfavourably. The Largest Island.—The late Bishop of Winchester, after distributing prizes to a Girls High School, put this question '.to the girls, and it took, them three minutes to answer ;—" which was the largest island, before Australia was discovered ?"—Some suggested Borneo others made More absurd answers, but at length a girl hit upon the correct reply "Australia." The Teaching of Geography and History.—Mrs. Clement Parsons When some time since addressing the Parents' National Educational Union, condemned the

A SERGEANT'S STORY.

Everyone is familiar with the rigid medical examina- tion which volunteers are called upon to pass by the military authorities before enteriag active service. Sergeant Kelly, 39, Stewart-street,HeatonNorris,Stock- por,t, says : _ _

- - - " When I was called up to join my regiment (4th Batt. Cheshire Regt.,) I was rejected on account of Kidney Trouble. I immediately looked about for a reliable Kidney Remedy and selected Dodd's Kidney Pills, which completely cured me. I again volunteered for service and passed the doctor all right and went out to South Africa, where I was at Bethulie Bridge for two years. I returned borne well and never since my cpre have I had the slightest trace of =my old trouble. Dodd's Kidney Pills are the right medicine for Kidney Trouble." Sergeant Kelly's experience should guide everyone who is suffering with Kidney Trouble in any form, to the use of Dodd's Kidney Pills a remedy which seems to have no equal for cases of this kind. Almost every day reports are being published of the most serious cases cured by Dodd's Kidney Pills.

A WIDNES CHRISTMAS ORGIE.

Fatal Sequa—A Severe Censure,

An inquest was held at the Widnes Police Court on Monday, by Mr. Samuel Brighouse, touching the death of John Henry Carden, boilermaker, living with his wife and family at :32 Terrace-road, Atha was found dead in the house 15, Charlotte-street, Widnes, on Christmas Day.

Sirs. Carden, widow of the deceased, stated that her husband left home about half-past six, after giving her his wages. He had about 12s. on his person and left saying that if he was not home by eleven o'cloa she was to_go.to bed.

Jitne Priug, a young married woman living with a Mrs. Wright, at 15, Charlotte-street, was next called. She said she met Carden at the Engineers' Arms. They were unficquainted before this meeting. Witness told deceased where she lived, and asked him to see her there later in the evening. At the same time she entered into conversation with two youths, William Yates, 46, Oaklands-street,, and Richard Duckworth, Peelhouse Farm, and also invited them to the house. She saw the three men as arranged. More drink was brought in, and although Carden was apparently sober when he left the hotel, he was drunk at this time. Staggering to get from a chair to the sofa, he fell on the floor, and was allowed to temain there all night, with only a pillow under his head. At half past eight -next morning Mrs. Pring entered the kitchen and fell over the body of deceased, in the same position as she had left him.

Mary Ann Wright, landlady of the house 15, Charlotte street, said she was away on Christmas Eve, and did not know of these " carryings on," and was astonished when she was told of the afiair. Dr. C. Hutchinson said he bad made a post-mortem examination, and found that congestion of the lungs, due to exposure through lying on a cold floor was the cause of death.

In reply to the coroner, witness stated that a little attention to the man on Mrs. Pring's part would probably have saved his life. The Coroner, in his sur..ming up, said, regarding the woman Pring, that she was at the bottom of it all, and responsible for the whole thing, as well as morally responsible for his death. She went to public-houses and made assignations with these men. As men of the world he and the jury knew well the purpose for which she invited them. Having drank the man's drink.' and helped to spend his money, the least she could have done, when he had rolled off the sofa, was to see that he was comfortably disposed for the night. The jury, in returning their verdict, said ,they agreed with the coroner, but thought that death was due more to exposure than to the drinking. They added that they felt that Mrs. Wright should be reprimanded for having a woman like Mrs. Pring in her house. They also stated that they thought that Mrs. Pring should not only be censured, but should, if possible, he legally proceeded against. The Coroner censured the women, and with regard to Pring. said she had caused the men named to spend their Christmas Eve in a beastly, indecent sort of way. She left the man on a stone floor in a bitter night, and he had died. He was sorry t• e could not advice that she was legally responsible, but she was morally so, and be hoped she would always remember that it was through her that this man came to this end.

The Examiner Saturday, January 2, 1904.

(Copyright.?

"Here is Alex ! " exclaimed Mrs. Leighton, with relief, as her daughter returned. , How handsome she looked; how even brilliant Leonie paled beside her, the mother truly thought. How could Eric have passed her by—if in his inmost heart he had—for such a woman as Leonia La Marque? What fool § men were! The two ladies passed out to the victoria, but Alex noticed that for one infinitesimal moment the Parisian paused on the threshold and glanced each way as one does to see who is •in the street. It might be Alex's fancy, but, if so, it was not the first time she had fancied that Leonie had done the 'same thing on leaving any building. Had Eric ever noticed it, too ? Few things escaped him as a rule. • "Regent-street," Mrs. Westcliffe bade the coach- man; and off went the dainty equipage with its pair of handsaue little horses: none but the finest would Eric own-

What wonder that many a passer-by looked after the carriage and its fair occupants, albeit Leonia carried her parasol low enough to shadow her face. She usually did. "The sun burns one's face so," she said. 'She could not think how Alex could go without a sunshade as she did."

"I love the sun, " 4aid Ale; "and my skin can take its chance. Ikwon't hurt, I daresay." "Oh, as you please, dear," rejoined Leonie, airily. But somehow Alex got a vague idea that the sun's gaze was not all Leonie wanted to keep off. In a few minutes Mrs. Westcliffe stopped the carriage before one of the shops—a large and fashionable one, be sure—and. the two ladies entered, the younger feeling already bored. In the course of the next hour spent there, Leonie expended a tolerably big sum of money in all sorts of fal-lals, an expensive ball dress included. She paid for the goods certainly, but laughingly said in French to Alex that the bill cleared her out of pin-money and Eric must give her more.

"What will he say ?" thought Alex, drearily. "He is not rich enough for such a drain if it is to he continued at this pace and so early begun.," When they left the shop she instinctively watched the Frenchwoman. Yes, again that slight pause and swift covert glance each way before she passed right out. This time Alex was quite ,certain of her own observation. "Where to now?" she said. "Oh, we can go into the Park for a turn or two."

The coachman therefore drove on and into Piccadilly, Leonie chattering and lobking along the shops to see if there was anything that caught her fancy. Suddenly she almost • startled Alex by suddenly exclaiming, sharply: "But what an odious crowd here! We shall never get on. Turn up that street, quick, jervis I " Jervis obeyed, secretly wondering, and Alex glanced behind her instinctively as the victoria swept round the corner. In that glance she just caught sight of a man at a window, a first-floor window of a restaurant in Piccadilly,. and the man was looking after them, Alex was almost sure. Was that the cause of Leonie's sudden anger, almost agitation? A sort of deadly sickness swept over the girl for a second, then she said, carelessly: "It isn't worse than usual."

"Yes, it is. And how hot it is ! I shall go home. Home, Jervis, and he shall take you on." "As you please," Alex answered, easily. She covertly watched her companion all, the way home, and saw that several times Leonia threw a quick, nervous glance behind, unnoticed, as she thought, no doubt, and when her own door was reached she was unmistakably relieved, Alex saw and felt. Heavy enough indeed was her own heart as she went on home, full of the worst fears for Eric's future, but she said nothing of it all to her mother. Somehow the knowledge of her own life- long love for her cousin held her silent. Yet more did that knowledge hold her silent to Eric himself. .r Alex could do was to watch and wait events.

Only a few weeks married, but was Erie West- eliffe's paradise still as cloudless an illusion as in the first week or two? The awakening began so soon and so insidiously that it had no form or thought or shape—it was a thing felt darkly, and even then to be put Away and thrust aside, but yet could not be so crushed. For even in these early days there had been slowly, dimly stealing into the man's soul a subtle sense of loss, of something wanting, of a change somewhere, a vague melting away of the dream in Which he. had blindly lived Nr so brief a space. Two days after that drive with Alex, Westcliffe was busy over letters in his study, a room at the end of the hall, when Leonia came in with a little tap-tap and apology as he glanced up. Half- laughingly, half with the prettiest of portentous manners, she went to him, and, laying her velvet cheek against his caressingly, said, pleadingly: "Don't scold poor little me for extravagance, mon ami, but I have spent all my money, my pocket money, and—"

"Spent all-Lin these few weeks!" repeated Eric, looking up in amaze, with the suspicion of a frown; and there was a curious, scarcely-conscious sense of shock, but all too intangible to grasp. He drew the lovely pleader forward, adding: "But how is it, Leonia? I don't understand it. None left!" "Ah, but now you are angry," said she, with tears in her voice. "I do not know how I did it, somehow. And now my dressmaker much wants her bill, and I do so want a small cheque to make it up. Just this time, cher Eric, and I shall make do next quarter like nice good girl." "1 am afraid you must. What do you call a little cheoue, eh ? What do you want for this bill?"

"Why only for twenty pounds," said Leonie, coaxingly. For a second Eric paused, then said with a half laugh, but serious meaning behind: "Well, you shall have it this time, but you must not think I am made of money, you know, my dear. If you are so wasteful in a few weeks, what will the end of a year be ? " She laughed and kissed him, declaring she would never be "so naughty" again. Westcliffe unlocked a drawer, took out his cheque- book and drew the asked-for cheque, which Leonie took with profuse thanks and bright smile. "I am going out shortly, too," she added, "and I will pay Madame Clernence myself." "Very well; come in to say good-bye as usual," Eric said. "Fain not too busy for that." She nodded gaily and fluttered away with the cheque for twenty pounds, and Westcliffe tried to bend his attention on the letters before him. He could not. His thoughts had Somehow got into a tangle with a dim, uneasy sense of gloomy prescience underlying the tangle. Still, he tried perforce and struggled on, but the minutes and quarters of the hour dragged on till an hour went by. "What an age Leonie was dressinc, just to go, out. Alex wouldn't be so long making a ball toilet."

"Haven't I been a long time ! " exclaimed Leonie, gaily re-entering, gloves in hand and carrying a handsome but very small handbag. Rather a forced gaiety, it vaguely crossed Eric. "Oh, peste, a ring at the door-bell! I shall wait till the person is gone." She sat down vexedly, putting the reticule on a chair next her and began to pull on a glove. The door opened, and the footman appeared. "If you please, sir, a clerk from your lawyer wishes to see you," he said. Westcliffe looked up with a flash of surprise. "She' him in here, he said; and Leonie rose quickly to burry away, out as the servant wont out the visitor, a genteel man, but quite a stranger to Eric, passed the footman into the room, and shut the door behind him.

"I beg your pardon, sir," he began, and LeOnie went deathly white, and a strange look of terror flashed into her eyes. "Are you Mr. Eric West- cliffe and that lady—your wife?" "Yes," said Erie, haughtily, just because he was in truth so startled.

The stranger was no lawyer's clerk, but was as surely-- "Exactly, sir," said the man, quietly, as if Eric had spoken. "I am a detective officer, and have a warrant to arrest Justine Sorel—that woman!" The man uttered a sharp exclamation, and fell back a step. It was the woman's face more than the officer's charge which wrung that cry of agony from his white lips. "What does this mean ? There is—there must be—some terrible mistake. This lady is my wife." "Yes, yes! Some terrible mistake!" echoed a hoarse, strangled voice from the woman that Eric could not have recognised. "It is a mistake of identity." "No mistake at all," said the officer, decisively, looking full in her ghastly face. "You have been wanted' for this kind of game these ten years, Madame Sorel."

_ " Ten years! "broke in Westcliffe, catching at a straw, unconsciously fighting desperately against the conviction of Lennie's own. face and manner. "Then there nans.t be some mistake. My wife was barely fifteen ten years ago." !Cho detective nearly smiled outright. "Lord bless you, sir, she's five-and-thirty if she's a day. And, pardon me, but luckily for you she is not your wife at all, as her first and only real. husband—Sorel—is alive. She deserted him, being poor, for this game. Entraps rich men into marrying

her, then robs, forges on them, and clears out, sir. You're the fourth or fifth victim, but the second one has never quite ceased to have her searched for. We got on her track in Paris a year ago, but lost it. Two days ago I was at a window in Piccadilly and saw her with a young lady in a victoria "—Leonie set her teeth—"but she had 'it driven down a side street—saw me looking hard— before I got out. I soon found out her where- abouts,:and—here I am, sir, in consequence." "It is all false! A lie!" cried the woman, fiercely. "And you dare ~to listen to it all, Eric!" "Hush!" he said, sternly, and his voice seemed scarcely his own. "Where is that cheque you asked me for an hour ago and were going now to cash ? Give it me, and confute this charge!" "I left it upstairs," she said, in the same hard, defiant way; but her hand made an instinctive movement towards her pocket as'both men saw. "It is in your pocket," said the detective, coolly, "You may as well give it up quietly, you know, since it must be found when you are searched at the station. Give it up, and that bag, too," taking up the well-filled bag from the chair. She looked furtively from one to the other with hard-drawn breath and set teeth, looking in those few minutes haggard and old. Then she suddenly snatched a purse from her dress and flung it at Erie's feet with the action and look of the baffled, savage animal she was. "The cheque is as you yourself drew it! Deny it if you dare!"

Silently Westcliffe picked it up, and took oa the cheque. _

Was Elie whole world falling away from under his feet? Was he in a hideous dream as his eyes stared in a dazed way on the paper his fingers slowly, mechanically put out to the detective, for there it stood out clear, as the mocking sunlight: "Pay to Mrs. Westcliffe two thousand pounds— Z 2, 000."

The exact reproduction of his own handwriting; the letters, the two added noughts, the forgery, the perfect work of a very skilled forger—who had also used, with a practised hand, the chemicals which entirely remove the original ink. That whole hour's interval; then, was grimly explained. She meant to cash this and—disappear. "I drew this for twenty pounds," he said, heavily. "You must do your duty, sergeant." , "Thank you, sir. I will now open this bag to further convince you." It had a spring, which he pressed, the woman watching from the chair on which she cowered rather than sat.

Out from the bag the officer drew parcels of silver paper, enclosing costly jewels—family heir- looms, some of them, diamonds, emeralds, rubies —which had a value for their owner that no money could_ have replaced. _ _

"There, sir—a nice loot she meant to make out of this latest—and last—adventure. Will you, too, prosecute ? " •

Erre drew back with a shiver.

"I think not, unless your charge fails." "It cannot fail, sir; we have dead proofs. It was only she herself who had eluded capture," was the answer; "only this case will doubtless have to be Mentioned as another proof of what she is. But you may choose to charge her with bigamy, for your own sake," he added. "Your marriage is null." Eric started.

"Of course. No, I can take other legal counsel about that," he said, hoarsely, and turned aside. "Do your duty." 111.2

Crash—shame—disgrace Made a fool of by a base adventuress, a lovely, worthless wormen ; it was bitterest gall, indeed, to the man whose pride and he-nour were so deeply wounded. Yet through it all he was startled by a dim sense of relief—ol being free from a weight—a sense painful, in ont way, that the wound did not reach his very heart and soul: that self-deception had been slowly Passing away before the climax of to-day shattered it to atoms.

It was hours before Eri6 could gather himseli up to face what he felt he must do: firstly, tell the truth to Alex and her mother. What would she—his sweet cousin—say, think? What would the world say of his late infatuation and its result ?

But he went round to Great Cumberland-street after the hour when any visitors would be there. He could scarcely have ever told exactly how 'he began, what he first said; he only knew that he broke the ice at once—abruptly even, and briefly, but holding fast the soft little hand which Ale, silently stole into his. "I knew lately," he said—" I got to know that I had made a terrible mistake; the idol was clay, indeed, as yore both thought—a miserable mistake."

"But, dear boy," said Mrs. Leighton, affectionately, "it is a thing to be deeply thankful for that this wretched impostor is not, your, wife— and never was. Heaven! If she had not had a husband He shivered'.

Alex said softly: "Don't let us think of that, mother I had half decided, Eric," she went on, to give him time to rally, "to tell you many little things I have seen in her ways, as well as about that drive. I saw the man at the window, too, and suspected that some climax was.at hand." "You did? But you were not such a fool as I," Eric said, bitterly. "You warned me of her at the first, Alex, only I was blind and deaf, and would not listen. I reap as I have sown. I shall go abroad for a while, and after that, I suppose, the gossip will have had its run. I shall see you both before I leave, of course. You are faithful and true whatsoever else , may fail," he said, unsteadily. Were they not. Did he then get a dim sense of all he had so nearly lost, but might, perhaps, now -regain?

• • The arr•est, under extradition, of• Justine Sorel, alias La Marque, alias Mrs. Westcliffe, created a sensation in London and Paris. In the latter capital she was presently tried and heavily sentenced for forgeries and frauds of a similar kind to that which had victimised Eric Westcliffe. He went abroad, shaken, disgusted with himself, and for many months no one at home saw him. Sick at heart, he kept away; but at length the old sweet memories, deep down, of Alex, drew him irresistibly back to her feet to plead for pardon, hope, love. "Blind fool—and worse than fool—that I hale been," he said, holding her hands like a vice; "but, anh, my darling, in pity don't tell me lam too late for even hope!" "Too late? Ah, no. dear Eric! Have I not always loved you only ? " whispered Alex, softly; and Eric folded her to his heart for always; there was no false glitter here—all was purest gold.

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

I don't fancy there has ever been a time before in the annals of dress when a girl had so many qpportunities of making a modest dress allowance go a long way by the aid of .a little practical knowledge of how to create out of odd lengths of silk, satin, and lace the many dainty etceteras of the toilette ' which every fashionable woman ~look upon as quite necessary adjuncts to a successful tout en- semble. It is in these details that the costli- ness of dress lies when purchased ready-made, as the dainty stitchery must all be done by hand; and so, by spending a few hours dialy which might otherwise be wasted on less pro- fitable employment, originality can be given

Blouses more particularly offer a wide field for all sorts of work, as the yokes and upper part of the sleeves of these usdul garments are in the best French models distinguished by fine tuckings, drawings, em- broidery, and lace insertions. her wealthier sisters who can afford to pay for skilled Ilabour. Cravats, of course,,. are another somewhat expensive item, but are necessary with the turnover little collar. They are not, however, difficult to make, and the straight collar-band of folded lawn or silk called " abbe " or " avocat," which takes its name from the two straight pieces which hang down the centre-front, is one of the simplest and most fashionable styles. The home worker should always avoid at- tempting anything that is ambitious, and rest contented with simplicity and neatness. The pretty blouse depicted is made of soft washing silk—which, by the way, should be of a fairly good quality, as otherwise it loses its look badly when washed. Soft silk stands hard wear better than chine, which soon cuts and rubs into holes. This is accounted for, we are told, by the quantity of tin used in its construction to give it ,stsbstance. A soft silk tucks and gauges better than the stiffer varieties. There are some very pretty blouse materials this season in the most delicate and rich colours, and a lengthof four or five yards of one or other is ever a useful and seasonable gift, as, if not required for immediate wear, it can always be laid aside for a few months without any risk of it losing its beauty or fashionableness_ dion-ple- d nuns-

GLITTER AND TRUE GOLD.

BY EDITH STEWART DREWRY, Author of "On Dangerous Ground," "Only an Actress," "For Somebody's Sake," "A Cruel Bondage," "Baptised with a Curse," &c.

"She is a fascinating woman, above all things," said handsome Eric Westcliffe, lazily pulling his moustache, but with 'a flash in his bright eyes that belied his apparent indifference. "Yes, that is the exact word for Madame La Marque. She is fascinating." "So everyone says,* • answered pretty Alex Leighton, his second cousin, just returned from the Continent. "She seems to have turned the heads of all you men already," she added, laughingly. "Who—or what—is she ? Where was she first met ? " "Oh, I believe she was first introduced by Lady Maude Sylvester; said she knew Madame La Marque well abroad." "o—h Is madame a widow? " said Alex. "Yes, a widow. This is her At Rome afternoon, by the way; so ta, ta ! She has a charming furnished flat." "Oh, of course," laughed Alex. "Everyone has that—'sharps and flats alike! Only the sharps don't pay the huge rents or anything else; and the flats pay—till the flat sends them into the Bank- ruptcy Court." Eric laughed as he rose. "You are satirical ; but it is too often true, certainly. Good-bye." "Good-bye; don't quite lose your head," was her merry retort. But when he was gone she sighed heavily. Only a few weeks ago had Lady Maude Sylvester—who, some unkind folks hinted, was impecunious— introduced the brilliant foreigner into her circle of wide latitude; and already the men were at the fair Circe's feet, to a more or less extent. She had such splendid eyes and exquisite complexion, such a charming voice and pretty foreign accent. She was so graceful, too, and young; for, though she had frankly told Lady Maude that she was five-and-twenty, she scarcely looked even that age—as the most censorious rival must have admitted. Besides all this, she was certainly a clever woman; she talked well, aid had evidently been much about and seen much of the world; indeed, she seemed to have crowded into her short life more experience than many possess at a decade more of years. She dressed in exquisite taste, but never too expensively. "I cannot afford the costly costumes, see you," she would say, laughingly, with charming frankness. "My money shall suffice for much comfort; but for the extravagances—non, non! I love it not, cher M. Westcliffe." - To whom this was said one evening at a theatre to which Eric had taken her. He was for the time—or believed himself to be—in Paradise. Of all her admirers, she certainly seemed most to favour this one—" handsome, attractive, and rich," added cynical rivals who envied her conquest. For unquestionably Westcliffe had quickly struck his colours to fascinating LeonieLa Marque, although previously a good many people had thought it would be a match between him and his beautiful cousin, Alexandra Leighton. Perhaps at the core his true fealty was there; but at five or six- and-twenty-,--indeed, at any time—most men are easily swept into an infatuation which, while it lasts, is a veritable madness; and Eric was no exception to the rule. "He is quite epris," a lady in the stalls said, glancing up at the box. "It is she with him, I think; she is half hidden by the curtain as she sits."

"She always sits so," was the reply; "does not like to' be stared at, perhaps "—with a slight sneer. _ _

The Other lady laughed. "She doesn't mind a thousand stares. She wants to play the retiring part, I expect, or else a refinement of vanity to arouse interest by, acting its antithesis." This might be so; but, whatsoever her motive, it certainly was the fact that when in any public place Madame La Marque never put herself en evidence, but rather the reverse. "Won't you take the middle seat ? " Westcliffe said, when, on entering the box, she took the chair nearest the curtain. "You will not see so well there."

--Madame La Marque gave him a bewitching smile, and answered: 'Thanks; but I see well here and prefer this place." "Your wish is law to your true knight," said Westcliffe, with a look ant intonation that gave meaning to the galanterie of the speech. "Oh, you flatterer!" laughed madame, lightly. "Is truth flattery? lam your true knight '—he bent down, his voice dropped to a whisper, full of the passion which carried all before it in he? presence. "You know it! You know I am your lover—who worships you!" "Hush, hush I" the woman said, hurriedly, under her breath—was it real or acted? "You must not love me! I am not worthy. I—l have nothing save--" "Dearest Leonie, r want nothing, ask nothing, but your heart," 'Erie interrupted, impetuously. "Don't tell me that I cannot win that ! "

--- Leonie glanced up, then dropped her eyes with a pretty flush. "Ah, but I must so tell you, because—you have won it already."

Lady Maude Sylvester was in an ecstasy over her protegie's conquest—her "sweet young friend ! " she gushingly exclaimed; but Mrs. Leighton said drily to an intimate friend: "She has never said where and how she first knew this too fascinating enslaver of Eric's senses." • "N —o. You think, then—" _ _

"A good many things, my dear. Lady Maude is very impecunious, I believe, also very foolish, gushing, and ready to accept people on their own ipse dixit—especially if bank-notes go with it. Good-bye. I can only hope that something will break it off." Vain hope! Nothing did so occur, and the marriage speedily took place. Lady Maude wanted a grand wedding, but the bride decidedly refused; neither she nor Westcliffe liked fuss. She did not even wish for any announcement in the fashionable papers; and was exceedingly vexed—or seemed so, at any rate—when such announcement did appear a couple of days before the wedding. "It is Lady Maude's doing, I know!" she exclaimed to Westcliffe, with such undue anget that he looked surprised—was, indeed, in a vague way, a little startled, as she quickly saw, and pulled herself up. "Bahl Now, lam stupid to be so vexed It does not matter."

They were married quietly, as she wished, and went away for a fortnight to a remote little seaside place, where the lover would fain have remained for much longer in his paradise of glamour. But the bride laughed gaily, and said she wanted the last month of the season. Se they returned to the furnished house Westcliffe had taken for that said month. And surely fascinating Mrs. Westeliffe ought to have been happy, if adulation, continual gaieties, and liberal pin-money make happiness. She took a great fancy to Alex Leighton; at any rate, she made much fusg with her in her effusive way, which to Alex seemed insincere and repellant; but she accepted things for what they seethed—it was only one more mask to wear, one more pain to bear for Eric's sake, whether for months, or years, or for life. One ramming Alex saw the pretty victoria turn into Great Cumberland-street, and pull up at their door; two mutates later Leonia cane rustling into the room in a dainty morning costume, "got up," certainly, regardless of expanse. She greeted Mrs. Leighton and her daughter in her usual effusive way. "I have come to fetch you, ,chere Alex," she exclaimed. "I want you to help nie in shopping, and eon, 'perhaps, to Christie's. Eric would not come."

"What, litoppiing ? Of course not!" laughed Alex, who hated it as much as he did, but did not quite like to ,refuse flatly. "I don't care for that job myself, Leorlie ; so--" "Oh, but—non, non, dear! You will be good- natured," .cried Lennie. '"I must have a new dress for Lady Maude's ball, see you, if only to do Eric credit," said she, laughing. Mrs. Leighton said, drily: "Eric's credit is scarcely involved, I think, in such a trifle, is it ?" Leonia laughed, and shrtigged her shoulders.

"Oh, he would not like me CO-be not well dressed —and for this. Go, Alex, get your hat, and come. You have the taste so exquisite in everything." "What can be her motive?'" thought Alex, noo_ at all flattered. But she went .away to get ready, whilst Leonie entertained—or worried—Mrs, .Laighton with a flow of gossipy chatter,

CONGREGATIONALISM IN GREAT

BRITAIN.

The return, to be issued in the forthcoming official "Year-book " of the Congregational Union will show considerable progress in several directions. The editor has failed to receive statistics from 66 chnrches, but over 4.500 in England and Wales have reported. These provided sitting. accommodation for 1,646,092 persons. The church .membership is set down at 418,108. as corn- pat ed with 4-1M,865 a 'year ago, a net increase of 4,243. In the Sunday schools ' are 63,077 'teachers, a. decrease of 227 ; while the number of Sun- day scholars has increased to 670,314, a clear gain of 3,809. The number of accr?dited lay preachers is 3,140. If to these returns are added those from Scot- land and _lreland, there 'is a grand total of 4,847 churches and mission stations, with accommodation for 1,750,138 worshippers. • The total membership is said to to!, 454,637—a clear increase of 6,374,the largest increase reported for some years. Of the 3,147 ordained ministers, 243 are described as " temporarily with- out pastoral charge," while there are 321 churches without pastors. There are 2L'B evan- gelists or lay pastors. There are 66,242 teachers and 704.745 scholars in the Congrega,tional Sunday schools of the United Kingdom. There are 441 students in the Taeological Colleges at home and in the colonies, with about 300. native students in the institutions belonging to the London Missionary SOciety. Of 89 new ministers who last year settled in the United Kingdom, the train- ing of eight is said to be "unknown." There are 487 ministers in the British colonies and on the Continent of Europe., In Canada there are 153 churches, in AuAralasia 370, in South Africa 314, in the West Indies 93,• and in Japan 107.

THE FAR EAST.

The eyes of the whole civilised world are at the moment upon Russia and Japan, and the ~ outlook appears very ominous. The tension has become very acute during the last month; and unless the Americkak-scheme for arbitration succeeds, or Russia climbs down, war seems inevitable. Japan with ler customary decision and promptness of action, has found herself , forced to deliver what is practically an ultimatum to her great rival in the East, and the answer is now being awaited with eager anxiety all over the world. 'Everyone is by now con- vinced that Japan will remain firm, and it is only a qw2stion as to whether Russia will yield or not. Japan demands the complete evacuation of Manchuria, which ought, under an existing agreement, to have taken place on October Bth, and, so far, Russia ,has refused point-blank. Russia is by no means cowardly, or a nation to sit down pleasantly under such a demand; but she is at present financially in a bad way. Her credit, also, is mortgaged to the hilt and her ability to borrow crippled by her recent large expendi- ture: and, as we know to our cost, a r modern war swallows up a mint of money. Again, Russia is in a very serious state internally, and is threatened with little short of a revolution, owing to centuries of govern- mental abuse. So far as we are concerned, though allied by treaty to Japan, we have 'little chance of becominc, embroiled in the -aflair,

japan find herself at war with two powers :at one time. This hardly looks likely, as France, who is Russia's ally on tile same terms, is unlikely to join in, especially as -we are ow upon the best of terms with •our Gallic neighbours. Of course, a war be- tween Russia and Japan would, to a large -extent, be naval, as the conditions on land .are unfavourable: and, furthermore, neither -side could, make much progress. The Russians, on paper, have a vast preponderance in both --naval and military forces : but this is levelled up by the fact that Russia cannot spare more than a small portion of her army, Tequiring so many soldiers for home defence. As nearly as possible, Russia would have 30 -warships against Japan's .22, while the latter 'could command 400,000 troops exclusive of 200,000 for home -defence, against 200,000 from Russia. So the fight if it takes place, -promises to be pretty even, if Russia is able -to sustain it by proper expenditure. The' advantage which Russia has upon the sea in point of numbers, is balanced by the better condition and superior class of the Japanese fleet. Though uubiassed in the matter, our ,sympathies as a nation cannot help inclining towards our ally and the younger power, .which has so .many points of similarity to 'ourselves.

MR. STEAD'S IDEAL.

• It seems that to Mr. W. T. Stead, the real pioneer of the now almost general "new" journalism, has been left the greater honour •of overcoming entirely that undesirable im- -personal element of the British daily paper, 'which has never, up to now, been assailed in .earnest. Mr.

===== 0002601_19040102_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
Stead to - day,

just as he was in his younger but not more militant days •of the "Pall Mall Gazette," is a people's journalist, and -sees the news- paper's funda- mental prin- ciple is a means .of unrestricted friendly inter- course between .editor, writers, and readers. .31r. Stead 'knows the old :anonymity -which many a journalist still -Cherishes as not only a privilege exclusive to his profession but its greatest charm, is held -in something very like contempt by the read- ,,er, for it is not in human nature to feel for the pet conserva.tisms of other people. Mr. •Stead w on i\londay next, embody his ideals in the "Daily Paper," and very ;anxiously is Loudon newspaperdom awaiting the experiment. The "Daily Paper" may be said to aim at being really the organ ,of a gigantic social club, with branches -where subscribers may pass away the idle hour. The paper is to be what is technically known as a localising one ; , one page in it will be devoted solely to the news ond advertisements appealing only to the -district where that fraction of the circulation is delivered, and at that particular district's branch many goods advertised for sale in the Journal can be inspected. At first the branches will be confined to Greater London. Mr. Stead's general scheme is ingenious, and -e3pells success,

MR. T. STEAD.

THE NEW MEMBER FOR LUDLOW.

The result of the Ludlow election is claimed as a success by both parties. That is to this extent—the seat has been retained by the Government, or rather by Mr. Chambvlain, as Mr. Rowland Hunt pronounced unre4rved- ly for the late Colonial Secretary s Preferential Tariff System;

The Tariff Reform League are. however, jubilant at this result, following as it does on th,: heels of Rochester, Lewisham and Dulwich, and go so far as to aver that, had it not been for the Tariff Reform plank of the elected candidate and his enthusiastic adhesion to Mr. Chamberlain's policy, the Government would have found themselves in the wrong box owing to their general un- popularity in the constituency. As the. new Member pointed out in his subsequent speech, the election was particularly important,, as it is the first really agricultural constituency in which Tariff Reform has been fought out. Mr. Rowland Hunt, Ludlow's new representative, is forty-five years of age, having been born. on March 13th, .1838. He was educated at Eton and Magdalene College, Cambridge, and is J.P. for Shropshire. He is an enthusiastic hunting man, and only recently resigned the Nlastership of the Wheatland Hounds. Going ant to the front during the recent war he served two years n Lovat's Scouts. The Ludlow election is his debit' in politics, and it has proved a most auspicious one. • -

The House of Cumberland is closely related to our own royal family, but for many years there has been, on the Cumber- land side, a feud. This was due to the ques- tion of succession to the throne of Britain. The Duke Of Cumberland at the beginning of the century was, a .younger son of King George 111., coming after Einf,- George IV., King 'William IV., the Duke of York, and the Duke of Kent. When King William was' on the throne and the other thee dead it was his hope that the young Princess Victoria would be passed over in his favour, and, being a man of violent and unreasonable temper, he never forgave her for supplanting him. This feud has taken many years to die out. The same Duke of Cumberland became King Of Han- over, but that dignity was lost some thirty years later. Another royal wedding is that of Princess Alice of Albany, and the date has been fixed for February loth. It will take Riace in St. George's Chapel, Windsor, and all the royal family will be present. On the same day the Duke of Norfolk, the Earl Mar- shal, will marry Miss Constable-Maxwell at Everingham Castle in Yorkshire.

The Examiner, Saturday, January 2, 1904.

TIIE FREE CHURCHES.

THE ARCHBISHOP'S LETTER.

The letters from the Archbishop of Canter- bury and the Bishops of London and Rochester show clearly enough that the clerical party is to make a great effort to keep possession of all the gains the Education Acts have . conferred upon them. The Archbishop, in his long letter, is very indignant at what he is pleased to term the misrepresentations of Noncon- formists, but his charge contains very few arguments, and those such as w,ill not hold water. For instance, he quotes an anonymous Nonconformist to say that the Bill imposes re- ligious tests upon teachers. And yet, if One is acquainted with the question at all, it will be seen that the statement is not so very ab- surd, for 'whereas formerly the .test whisch un- doubtedly was. imposed upon teachers in "voluntary" schools was the test of the Church, imposed by private individuals) under the new Acts the teachers become civil-servants and their salaries are paid by the State. Hence the State now 'imposes this religious test, and it is to this that Nonconformists and citizens generally most strenuously object.

===== 0002601_19040102_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
A CLERICAL CALL TO ARMS.

The Bishops of London and Rochester, in a circular issued recently, sound an urgent call to arms to all Churchmen to rally to the de- fence of the Education Act. Free Churchmen must see now that the fight is to be a long one and that they have to reckon with the active and organised opposition of the officials of the Church. "ENCOURAGING NONCONFORMISTS."

One of the statements of the Archbishop of Canterbury needs comment. Re says that "with ,a view to removing any possible diffi- culty . . . in single school areas, we are encouraging the appointment in large schools of one or more Nonconformists among the as- sisant teachers." A grim commentary on the words of the Archbishop is to be found in the columns of the "Schoolmaster," week by week, where numerous cases occur of advertisements for assistant teachers who must be members of the Church of England. Even in the Arch- bishop's diocese, and within a few miles of Canterbury, occur instances where this arbi- trary rule is laid down and Nonconformists are unjustly deprived of the opportunity of serving their country in the schdols maintained by the State.

FREE CHURCH CAMPAIGN FUND. These things make all the more necessary the appeal which the National Free Church Council is issuing for a fund to fight the next General Election in the interests of Education. Already many subscriptions have been re- ceived, but it is to be hoped that a still larger measure of support will be given to the fund. It is only by organisation that elections can hope to be won. Tariff reformers and Free Fooders are spending large sums in placing their Views before the country. In this fiscal warfare the gross injustices of the Education Act must not be forgotten, and Free Church- men must see that if they do not want the clerical yoke fastened on them for a :genera- tion or two, they must make one supreme effort now. Contributions towards the fund are being received by the Rev. Thomas Law Memorial Hall, London, E.C.

RISING YOUNG NONCONFORMISTS. It is often asked what. will become of the nation and the churches when the present leaders have disappeared, but it only needs a discerning eye to see among the rising young men in the State .and in the churches those who will be able to give no uncertain lead. A short account of Mr. Campbell's successor, the Rev. J. G. Stevenson, appears in this month's "Free Church Chronicle." The writer, Mr. Herbert D. Williams holds that whilst Mr. Stevenson is very unlike his predecessor, Mr. Campbell, he will nave a large amount of suc- cess. Mr. Stevenson has from the early days of his Essex pastorate been actively associated with the Free Church Federation movement, and no doubt in his wider sphere we shall hear a great deal more of him. F.MPLOYMENT OF BARMAIDS. The temperance question is likely to be a very prominent one in the near future in view THE NEW ISOLATION HOSPITAL. of Mr. Balfour's pledge to the Liquor party. HULL V. LEIGH. At Hull, before 8000 spectators. Johnson kicked a penalty goal for Leigh, .4nd Thompson equalised, the sides crossing over level lit the interval. In the second half Caryl', and then Harrison, scored for Hull, Brown converting one try. Taylor,the Hull back, retired hurt, but Leigh could not make, any impression. Roberts scored, Johnson goaling. Result—Hull, 2 goals 2 tries (10 points) ; Leigh, 3 goals 1 try (9 points). • OLDHAM v. HULL KINGSTON ROVERS. . I At Oldham. .The home side obtained a lead of 5

The clerk read a communication from the Local Government Board extending the Infectious Diseases (Notification) Act to chicken-pox, at the request of the Council.

points just before the interval through a score by Dixon, improved by Carpenter. In the second half Spottiswoode scored splendidly for Oldham. Starke scored for Hull, and Wilkinson scored a third try for Oldham, all of which were unimproved. Oldham showed greatly improved form on recent matches, and were easily winners by 11 points to 3.

THE FINANCES- AN OLD ACCOUNT.

Mr. Wright reported that there was a balance in hand at the last meeting of .ZBl5 16s sd. The receipts during the. month amonnted to £722 13s 7d, and the cheques drawn that day totalled £712 Os 2d, leaving a balance of £826 9s 10d.

The statement was approved. Mr. Wright said they had received a rather peculiar account from Dr. Robinson, of Runcorn. It was for the notification of infections diseases and dated as far back as 1890. The total amount was ,Cl.l 7s. 6d., but all the Council was really liable for was .12s. 6d. He asked for instructions on the matter. Mr. Linaker said that seeing that Dr. Robinson was so universally respected and was so good to the poor people he thought that if the account was a correct statement of his charges against the Council it should be paid in full. If necessary, the permission of the Local Govern- ment Board should he obtained.

It was decided to pay the account, on the amount being certified correct by the Clerk. The Council, how- ever intimated that in future accounts would be kept strictly within the legal limit.

THE EMPLOYMENT OF CHILDREN ACT. Dr. Adams reported the receipt of a letter from the County Medical 0 ricer of Health with respect to this Act, asking the doctor to furnish a list of occupations and the names of employers in the district who were engaging children in an'y labonr. He remarked that it was a large order, but would do the best he could to carry it out.

This was all the business of public interest.

===== 0002601_19040102_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
FESTIVITIES AT THE WORKHOUSE The Inmates and Officers Enjoy Themselves.

Them have been several interesting gatherings at the Workhouse this Christmastide. The inmates were pro- vided with excellent Christmas fare on Christmas bay and thoroughly enjoyed themselves. At the close of the dinner of roast beef and plum pudding, The MasTER addressed the gathering. It was not his 'intention, he said, either to speak from a text or to deliver a sermon, but for once in the course of a year he would call them "ladies and gentlemen." (Laughter and applause.) During the past ten years he had been in various workhouses on a similar occasion to the pre • sent, but in none of the 60 unions in England and Wales had the inmates enjoyed their dinner more than the inmates had done that day in the Warrington Work- house. (Applause.) None of them, however base, would like to retire to smoke the pipe of peace and contentment without expressing their thanks to the Guardians for their kindness in providing such a sumptuous dinner, andhe asked them, therefore, to give three hearty cheers for the Board. Three hearty cheers were then raised. Councillor ASHCROFT replied on behalf of the Board, and said he was sure he expressed the opinion of all present when he said how deeply they regretted the absence of Mr. Hickman, who had invariably delivered a humorous 'speech on that occasion, • but during the year his wife had died. They sympathised very sin- cerely with him in his bereavement. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Ashcroft concluded by proposing a vote of thanks to the Master and Matron and the staff for their ser- vices on that occasion, and spoke highly of the kindness of Mr. and Mrs. Awbery to the inmates. The motion was seconded by the Rev. T. W. BARBER, supported by the Rev. C. J. EDWARDS and the Rev. W. BRACECAMP.—The vote was carried by steady and con- tinued clapping of hands, similar to the well-known Kentish fire.

The MASTER acknowledged the compliment in a few well chosen.wordsAnd asked the inmates to show their appreciation of what had been done for them by excel- lent conduct during the day. IVlrs. AWBERY was also called upon to respond. She said her husband had adequately expressed his thanks and her own. She was much obliged to them.

Later in the thy, Councillor Ash-croft a-n-cf his family gave an entertainment for the benefit of the inmates.

STAFF DINN,KR.

The staff devoted all their energies on Christmas Day for the benefit of the inmates, but on Monday evening the hard worked officials had their innings. A staff dinner provided by the Guardians—the first of its kind at the Workhouse took place and was presided over by the master (Ar. Awbery). Several Guardians attended including Mr. A. J. Jolley, Mr. George Pilling, Mr. William Houghton, Mr. Thomas Halton. Amongst the officials present were Rev. W. Bracecamp, Dr.Peacocke, Mr. Shearer and about 40 of the staff.

• After the usual loyal and patriotic toasts had been drunk, the toast of "The Warrington Board of Guardians" was proposed by Mr. B. Shearer. He ex- pressed the hope that it would not be the last occasion on which the Guardians and officers would assemble at the festive board. He felt, sure that every officer serv- ing under the Warrington Board, whether indoor or outdoor, had always received at the hands of the Board justice, consideration and fair-play and that they con- scientiously and efficiently discharged their duties. He made allusion to the fact that upon that day, the 28th of December, 23 years ago, he was appointed an officer of the Board.—The names of Messrs. Pilling, A. J. Jolley, William Houghton and Thomas Halton were coupled with the toast. Mr. GEORGE PILLING, in reply, said the Board had been very fortunate in the selection of their officers and he did not think any institution of a similar kind was better staffed. Six months ago he and Mr. Brooks visited several workhouses for the purpose of selecting a master and matron and the Board had had no reason to regret the choice they made. (Hear, hear.) it was a pleasure to the Guardians to visit the house and see the • many improvements which had been effected for the comfort and benefit of the aged poor. Turning his at- tention to the hospital,Mr.Pilling said it was an institu- tion of which they were very proud.He thought they had in the superintendent nurse an officer well qualified to discharge the onerous dais appertaining to the over sight of the care and treatdltant of the sick. . Mr. WILT,Lisi HOUGHTON also spoke. He alluded Ito the step taken by the Board 20 years ago when they 'established Padgate Schools. He thought that depar- ture in poor law administration, to which many objected at the time, showed the prescience of the Guardians at that time,for many Boards of Guardians were now taking the step which was taken by the Wiirrington Board twenty years ago. (Hear, hear.) Mr. A. J. Jolley and Mr. T. Halton also responded. The toast of" The indoor and outdoor officers of the Board" was proposed by Mr. George Pilling and re- sponded to by the Rev. W. Bracecatnp, Dr. Peacocke and Mr. Shearer.—Mr. A. J. Jolley then proposed in felicitous terms the toast of" The Masterand Matron," and the Master briefly responded. The gathering was a very pleasant one and pleasing i break n the exactineduties of the staff. On Wednesday evening, a staff dance as held in the hospital. The corridors and rooms had been tastefully decorated for the occasion;and the scene as reflected by the van-coloured lights was a fairyland of delight. The introduction of palms and fairy lamps heightened the effect. Amongst those present were Mr. Brooks (chairman of the Board), Mrs. Clay, Mr. A. J. Jolley, Dr. Braide, Dr. Peacock°, Dr. Bowden, Dr. Eades (Winwick), Mr. and Mrs. Awbery (master and matron) Miss Biggin (superintendent nurse), and the staff. A varied programme of dances was gone through, and the dancing was agreeably reli‘ 7ed by vocal and instru - mental items.

===== 0002601_19040102_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
RAILWAY ELECTRIFICATION.

The electrification of the Liverpool, Southport, and Crossens portion of the Lancashire and Yorkshire Railway Company's system, the success or failure of which will probably spur on or retard similar enter- prises in all parts of the country, is now almost com- pleted. A statement issued from the central offices of the company in Manchester on Wednesday shows that the double line, 23 miles long, will be in full working order early in the new year. The first experimental run with a new four car train was made on Tuesday between' Southport and Formby, and proved entirely satisfactory The trains are all to be of the four car pattern, and the cars sixty feet long and ten wide.

The late Mr. William Davies, ot Oaerblaidd,who ha.f. just died, had a wonderful memory. Amongst his feats was the committal to memory of the whole of Wa,lker's Dictionary.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Confirmation at St. Paul's Church. Bishop Royston conducted a confirmation service in this Church on Sunday afternoon when there was a good congregation. Forty eight candidates werepresented for confirmation, one of whom came from St. George's Altrincham, and one from St. Faith's, Waterloo. The Bishop addressed words of counsel to the candidates, and after the laying on of hands he delivered another address. The clergy present were the Revs. W. Brace- camp (vicar), and T. W. Barber and C. J. Edwards, assistant clergy. _ .

_ Bold-street Wesleyan Church. A Christmas carol service was held in this church on Sunday evening, and was largely attended. The preacher was the Rev. G. H. Carnson. The choir ren- dered in highly creditable manner 'Good Night," "0 thou that tellest," and "For unto trs " ; and the carol hymns, "0 come, all ye faithful," " Hark, what mean these holy voices ?" and "All hail the power," were sung with fervour. Mr. Levi Richardson sang " Comfort ye and "Every valley," with good effect; and Nazareth " was well interpreted by Mr. Pearce. "0 thou that tellest "was rendered by Miss Pemberton. The organist, Mr. Frank Hickman, played the accom- paniments without the aid of an orchestra and conduc- tor, and he is to be complimented upon the complete success of the service. The offertories were on behalf f the Society Fund. Go!borne-street gaptist Church. The annual social gathering in connection with the adult Bible Class was held in the schoolroom , on Boxing Day when over 100 persons sat down to an ex- cellent tea. On Sunday evening the church was crowd- ed, when the choir, under the leadership of Mr. T. Brough, gave selections from the " Messiah "including "And the glory," "Behold the Lamb of God," "Lift up your heads,' " Worthy is the Lamb," 'Hallelujah." Mr. Harry Berrey, who was in good voice, sang with taste and expression " Comfort ye," " Every valley," and" Thou elidst not leave." Miss A. Woodland's rich. contralto voice was heard to advantage in "He shall . food His fleck," Miss E, 'Miller, and Mrs. W. H. Cotterill acquitted themselves ma creditable manner. Mr. -Albert Trow ably acted as accompanist. To- • morrow (Sunday) evening the Pastor will preach hhi annual sermon to young won and women. Wycliffe P. 8.4, Society,

A well attended meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A Society was held on Sunday. Mr. John Smith, of Warrington presided and based a brief address on the words Godliness is profitable unto all things, having promise of the life that now is and of that which is to come." (I Timothy, 4c, 8v). He showed that Godliness had given to the world the best things that it possessed Mr, E, Johrocm, B, A, ef Manchester, delivered an address in which he stated that the first thing to con- sider in life was its true end, its highest and 'ultimate destiny. If life was to prove satisfactory to them they must from its very beginninn- contemplate its end and aim. No life could as truly successful that was lived at random, and was dis- jointed and purposeless. He proceeded to show that they had the ideal life depicted in the life of Christ. Some men, he said, gave nothing, they consunied every- thing except the smoke of their own perverse influence. The true life was a lif3 of loving service.—Mr. B. Farrar announced that Mr. Gardner, of Padgate, a member of the brotherhood, had passed away. it was decided to forward a voteof condolence to the bereaved

ACCIDENT AT IRLAM LOCKS. A serious accident occurred at irlam Locks on Wed- nesday. Patrick Curley (29), a sailor, employed on the Barrow Castle, got his foot entangled in a repo as the vessel was being placed into the locks, with the result that his right foot was torn off. He was conveyed to Manchester by train and from the Central Station to the Royal infirmary by horseambulance. His condition is serious.

THE EXAMINER.

JANUARY 2nd 1904.

The New Year which NEW YEAR GREETING. made its bow 37ester.

day finds us as befits a vigorous nation in strenuous endeavour. Pro- fessor KARL PEARSON has affirmed a decline in the number and quality of great intellects, but there is certainly no diminution of energy or of ambi- tion. The young man cr miss who started a diary on Thursday evening will compare favourably with the older generation, who know how abruptly such records end. It may be that both pulpit and Parliament have, to some extent, teansferred their power in recent years to that intangible influence —the Press; and also that newspapers in their turn are dealing less reverently and more pertly with the things that matter. But, after all it must be admitted that the public never had a wider appreciation of mundane move- ments, Landon received familiarly during the past year two Kings, and with still greater demonstration of friendliness the President uf the French Republic. At least one Emperor and one other King are expected on our shores within the next few months. The rulers of the earth enter- tain each other with cheer. It is true that war- clouds cist unpleasant gloom over the inter- national courtesies and tokens of goodwill, but the very prolongation of the strain shows that diplo matists are taking a more serious view than they used of their duty. And it is not a small thing that, after the European cynicism against as in the war, the great compliment for peace-striving, the Nobel prize, should be in procoss of delivery from a foreign jury to an English Member of Parliainent.Thus the signs of the times—irregular, disjointed, and often in contradiction as they may seem, are towards order, and relief of unnecessary misery and distress. Some day, when the fighting powers of the different nations may be almost as easily estimated as the capacity of well-known I chess-players, there will be an end of war. The peoples of the earth will recognise that it is not worth the cost to produce a calculable result. That at least is the belief of those scientists who venture to indulge in prophecies. Meanwhile, we must be glad that the areas of friendly rivalry are exciting much attention, and that so many persons are pursuing them with zeal. The English students at Charlottenburg, and the German students that Mr. RHODES'S dead hand places at Oxford, will learn, among other things, to appraise bombastic utterances at their

net value, and, on returning to their countries, do their best in correcting obliqueness of national vision. We may hope, at the same time, that healthy emulation will go on, whether it be between individuals, provinces, or countries. 31ILLAis' picture of the old sailor hearing from his daughter of the North-West passage, and quietly saying: "It ought to be done, and England should do it," is the sort of sentiment that should stir British hearts, not only on New Year's Day, but on all days of the year. It represents the responsibility to go forward consequent on our splendid heritage.

A great deal of nonsense has been written by the so-called Belgian Shakespeare and others about our childish ignorance at the beginning of the twentieth century. "We may ask ourselves," says Maeterlinck in one of his essays, " whether our extraordinary unfitness for knowing a thing so simple, so incontestable, so perfect, and so necessary as the future would not form one of the greatest subjects for astonishment to an inhabit- ant of another star who should visit us." That sort of matter does to pair oft against the extravag- ant opinions that another class of thinkers enter- tain of man's advance. Our scientists are still divided whether we can get consumption from cow's milk ! Not only the origin but the means of treatment in some developments of the greatest scourge among diseases—cancer—remains a riddle to the disciples of Hippocrates ! On the other hand, science has been bar nessed to innumerable uses, not the least important of which is signalised to-day in the grant of a larger charter to motor- cars to lessen space by bringing people nearer to each other in point of time. The world is yet figuratively speaking, a great lumber place—one thinks of it as like an international exhibition at Paris or elsewhere a few weeks before the proper time for starting. -Much is in working order ; much inert; many of the people with only the dimmest conception of the forces around them That is true of civilisation to-day. We must be glad that the rac3s are ptepared to cultivate the policy of the open mind. We must warn them against that which may be rudely described as "the policy of the open mouth." Its most dangerous symptoms are seen in the revolting growth of charlatanry in the United States. The world is still in a great muddle, and its kindest philosophers can only, if they are honest, recall SWINBURNE-S words:

T have love at least, and have not fear, sad part not From thine unnavigable and wingless way; Thou tarriest, and I have not said thou art not, Nor all thy night long have denied thy day. - -

The mixture of light and darknesb, the glimmer- ings of intuition, the small certainties, the elaborate mechanical toys with which mankind amuses itself, and the fine youthful heroisms maintained by some to the grave—these things epitomise the gaiety and shade of life, and also its means of confidence. In endeavouring to regard ourselves and the wisest of our kind as bright-eyed or at least zestful children, anxious to make the most of our time, we certainly should not neglect prudence an economy as well as "neighbourliness "—the word which, after all, sums up the evangel. There is in GEORGE Gissixo's last inimitable work—" The Journal of Henry Ryecroft "—a wholesome piece of advice in this respect. He reminds us that the vulgar saw, "Time is money," when turned round reveals a precious truth—" Money is time." With money can be bought the cheerful and profitable use of hours which otherwise would be a terrible burden. Money enlarges life, though here again the general possibility is subject to many excep_ tions, and it is a happy thing that only a moderate amount of money is required to get a reasonable benefit out of life, that is, if expensive habits are not so grafted as to become second nature. In that spirit of modest hope and resolute industry we say to the new year, "Welcome, little stranger."

The troubles of the Free Food LIGHT FROM League should appeal even to LIVERPOOL. the coldest heart. There is a ptthetic situation at Liver- pool. The DUKE of DEVONsHIRE is to address a 'meeting there, anl Sir Wir,ul.ll FORWOOD, ch tir man of the local branch of the League, has written a letter of remonstrance to Sir JOHN WILLOX,WhO has declined the felicity of listening to the DUKE. Sir JOHN WILLox is a strong supporter of the •:11-overnrnent, and treats the DUKE as an enemy. painful to Sir WILLIAM This misconception is FORWOOD, who protests his loyal adhesion to Mr. BALFOUR. "I would ask who are the loyal sup - porters of the Government—those who, like the Unionist Free Food Leaguers, support Mr. BALFoun in his policy so far as he has developed it, or those sh are rushing in mad haste, without thought, after Mr. CIIAMBERLAIN and his policy.' True, the DUKE of DEVONSHIRE left the Cabinet— rather a singular way of showing his loyalty to the head of it; but Sir WlLLism FORWOOD dis- missee that as allittle'misrinderstanding.The Duke "resigned because he feared -Mr. BALFOUR in his Sheffield speech meant more than he said," Mr. BALFOUR has "repudiated that inference," so it is quite clear now that he and the Dike are of one mind. Moreover, the Duke's advice to voters " is not only quite consistent with loyalty to the Unionist party, but it is the only way the Govern- ment can be maintained in office"; for, adds Sir WILLIAM Foßwoon with brilliant sagacity, "if we are te vote for Unionist candidates pledged t 3 Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S policy it simply means that sooner or later—probably sooner—Mr. CHAMBERLAIN must step into Mr. BALFJUR'S shoes." Tae Dike, then, is bent on saving Mr. BILFOUR:i shoes for their lawfal possessor; but, strange to say, that misguided man shews no sense of this devotion. He blesses Mr. OnAmeEiti.a.lN's candidates at by-elections, and persists in regard- ing them as menit)ee3 of the " Ministerial party." He hails the return of Dr. RUTHERFOORD HARRIS and Mr. HUNT as a Unionist triurnph,and fails altogether to see how their defeat by Union- ist electors, following the advice of the Dike, could have illustrated the "on'y way" to keep the Government in office. It is certainly difficult to understand how the election of Liberal candidates instead of M. CHA.MBER.LAIN'i friends can strengthen Mr. BALFOUR'S han is; but this puzzle seems to present n, diffiialties to the Liverpool branch of the nee Food League. Will the Duke adopt Sir WILLIAM FORWOOD'S logic ? Will he say to the Liverpool Tories : " We are all resolved to keep Mr. BALFOUR in office, and the only way to do it is to vote Liberal whenever the Unionist candidate avows himself a supporter of Mr. CHAM- BERLAIN " ? At the Queen's Hall the Duke went so far as to say that he regarded Mr. BALFOUR'S policy with grave misgiving. He must surely have discovered by this time that the Prime Minister is acting in concert with Mr. CHAMBERLAIN, and that to defeat the one it is necessary to defeat both. This being the

lobvious deduction of common sense, how is it to be reconciled with the fundamental principle of the Free Food League—that Mr. BALFOUR'S policy is to be maintained against Mr. CHAMBER- Lar,;'s ? Mr. BALFOUR, after all, is Leader of the Unionist party, and it is absurd to keep up the pretence that he can be supported against him- self. While he is marching with Mr. CHAMBER-. LArx, and the Free Food contingent is marching in the opposite direction, how can the Dake cry: We are the Unionist party ; shew your loyalty to Mr. BALFOUR by following us "P It is suffi- ciently clear that the Unionist party is not going the way of the Free Food League, and the time has come for that body either to distinguish or frankly to cast in its lot with the Opposition. This is the dilemma in which the Duke will be placed at Liverpool by the action of Sir WILLIAM FORWOOD. He must disabuse that simple gentle- man's mind of the extraordinary notion that Unionist Free Traders can back Mr. BALFOUR and oppose him at the same time. If this is not made clear at Liverpool, the Free Food Leaguers will drift into Mr. CHA3IBERLAIN'S battalions.

Councillor John Maginn is nothing if not energetic. That he conclusively proved at the first meeting of the Town Council after his election when he:attempted to re-model the inquiry scheme —the product of two years' agitation and delibera- tion. Now he is prepared to tackle Warrington Bridge and throw in a couple of extra bridges while he is on the job. In the course of a letter published in another column Councillor Maginn condemns the suggested widening by cantilevers practically without a hearing. At any rate he advances no argument worthy of the name against Alderman Smethurst's proposal, and is at the same time, quite wrong in supposing that "the bridge will be an obstruction to navigation as soon as the new river diversion is completed and the surface of the water has been raised by the dam." It is not intended to raise the level of the water at Warrington bridge. This could not be done without causing flooding of the lower portions of the town, and anxious property•owners have already been publicly assured that the dam will not have any such effect. The canti- lever scheme is not ,luite so ridicu- lous as Councillor Magnin would make out. The bulk of his letter is however, devoted to the advocacy of a bridge across the river at Howley, connecting that part of the town with Latchford and opening out Victoria Park. This is another old scheme revived, but there is no reason why the practicability of carrying it out at some early date should not be again discussed. If Warrington develops at all it will have to develop on the south side of the river, and some such bold scheme as the one at Howley would, we believe, tend to immediate expansion. The Howley bridge has much to recommend it, but we cannot say as much for the one Councillor Maginn suggests at Walton. We must learn to walk before we run, and when we have walked over the widened War- rington Bridge and the new one at Howley then we can cast our eyes Walton way with some show of reason.

There is an interesting motion on the agenda for the Town Council meeting on Tuesday, which stands in the name of Alderman Bennett. It is as follows : "That any support which the Cor- poration may decide to give to the Manchester Ship Canal Bill be conditional upon an extension of the time for the construction of the Warring- ton Dock." The Bill in question is the one which includes the construction of the proposed works at the Bank Quay bend of the river, and the question o :supporting it is to be considered at a further cm. ference of manufacturers and traders. Alderman Bennett appears to us to be opening up a wide subject. Under certain conditions a dock was to have been constructed at Warrington within three years from the opening of the Ship Canal, and rose-coloured were the mental visions of Warring- tonians in those days. But the dock failed to materialise, and since then a portion of , the old river bed, which would, presumably have been absorbed by the dock, has been, and is now, in process of filling up. There is still a magnificent site for docks in Arpley fields and along the canal from Wilderspool to Walton, and the resurrection of the idea by Alderman Bennett is a small sensation in itself. Whether he will he able to tack the condition he proposes on the Corporation's support of the Ship Canal Bill is a moot point. We shall watch with interest the development of the Alderman's scheme—or dream—at Tuesday's meeting.

It is just ten years since the Manchester Ship Canal was opened. On New Year's Day, 1894, Latchford Locks were the starting point of a flotilla of gaily—bedecked veiseis on their maiden voyage along the canal to Manchester. At ten o'clock the white steam yacht Norseman led the way towards Manchester, followed closely behind by the Helvetia, which had been placed at the dis- posal of the representatives of Warrington by Messrs. Monks Hall and Company. In the 'rear was a long string of Mersey ferry steamers, tugs and cargo boats, the last of which had not arrived at their berths in the new Manchester docks when darkness closed the short winter day. On turning to our newspaper files recording this auspicious event we find that Alderman Francomb expressed the opinion that "the Canal would mean a lot of good for Warrington, and he hoped those on board would share in it." We are now able to look back on this hopeful prediction in the light of experience and it is somewhat depressing to know that it has not been realised. Manchester has reaped a golden harvest from the construction of the Canal but to Warrington nothing seems to have fallen but the Dead Sla fruits of costly litigation.

For ten years we have had one of the finest waterways in the world lapping our shores, but we have not yet a single dock in which to berth a vessel. Nor is there one in contemplation. Alder- man Bennett's motion is almost one of despair. Our traders seem satisfied to make use of the river with its limited possibilities insteadof tackling the greater and inure vital question.

The Museum Committee has reconsidered and reported upon the question of Branch Libraries, and recommends that a beginning be made with five delivery stations situated in shops at an esti- mated cost of £2O per year for each station. ,Issue Centres in elementary echools are also recommen- ded, and tha opening of an evening reading room at Silver-street schools at a cost of not more than £52 per year is advised. The question appears to have been carefully and completely investigated, and we presume that the committee's recommendations will be approved. The committee certainly cannot be charged with being reckless or over venturesome, but they have recommended a practical and useful scheme for bringing reading facilities within easy reach of a much greater number of inhabitants than ever be- fore. A year's experience will be most valuable and we hope to see the project develop to such an extent that reading rooms and lending libraries will soon become a necessity and a desideratum in all parts of the town. A cultivation of the love of rea ling among the masses will bring decided benefits to the community in its train. All credit to ex-Councillor Upson for the in ception of the idea shortly to be put in force. If the police records are to be taken seriously, and we presume they are—Warrington people have been on their best behaviour,in public, at any rate, this Christmastide. Not one solitary "drunk.' was brought before the magistrates on Boxing Day and the week-end batch of prisoners which awaited trial on Monday was smaller than usual. Unless summonses are to be issued, or some kindly disposed " Roberts " have closed their eyes to little deliquencies, we shah have cause to think that Warrington is pro grossing. along the path of sobriety. Christmas celebrations and excessive drinking too often go
