The appalling disaster at Chicago comes as a fresh call to local authorities responsible for theatre licences. There has been much irritation in London over the demands of the London ,County Council, and theatre managers affirm that some of the rules are harsh, and that the com- mittee is unbending. But there is another side to this question. Managers should not overlook the value of a testimoniAl from a censorious critic. If the London County Council gets a name for sternness, and its inspectors pronounce London , • theatres very safe, the managers will recoup I themselves their expense. It is not merely that nervous people will not be afraid to go to the theatre—their number is small, and scarcely worth Considering; it is that a crowded house may be hardened to stand an alarm. No number of doors I can afford security against loss of life from a mad, insensate stampede. It would be necessary that the walls should open. But if people know that there are effective means of holding flames, that the inflammable material is small, and that the means of egress are sufficient and sure the catas- trophes should be averted. The holocaust just reported will not turn pleasure-seekers from amusements even in a city so given to fires as Chicago. Private interests must fade into insigni- ficance in comparison with the interests of the community if they clash. But firmness here is for the good of all. Local authorities in face of such a disaster cannot ignore their duty with light hearts. But they can make their demands in a reasonably economic spirit. Rules as to gang- ways, fire-curtains,and outward-opening doors need not involve a great cost.

"Abolish the Horse Guards ! " It seems like saying,l" Away with Westminster Abbey! " or "Down with the Bank ! " Yet such is the cry which military critics possessed of the spirit of utilitarianism are raising. The Horse Guards, they say, is no longer the head-quarters of the Army. Secretary for War, Commander-in-Chief, Headquarters Staff are all at Pall Mall. Why then keep up the force of the Horse- Guards? Why mount guard there every day with splendid troopers condemned to sit on their horses half in and half out of dingy boxes in rain or sun? Why indeed? Only because the Briton dearly loves his national institutions, and the Horse Guards is one of them. Think of the the, nay, millions of Britons who have gained their first conception of the splendour of Imperial power When gazing awestruck at the stalwart figure irk shining cuirass and helmet with its Wondrous plume sitting motionless outside that gate! Think of the hearts that have been lost to those statuesque troopers! Whitehall, the seat of the Government of the most glorious empire the world has seen, is none too impressive itself, and its chiefeat glory would have departed were the daily guard to disappear. The mere sugges- tion is enough to make every nursemaid rise in revolt, and every country cousin unite to turn out the Government which would propose so great a desecration! True, the Guards guard nothing in Particular. Mr.Cardwell left nothing for them to -look after when he transported the Commander- in-Chief and all the Army Stiff, "bag and baggage," as Lord Wolseley observes, "to that un- healthy and curious congeries of houses in Pall Mall known as the a.War Office." The Horse Guards is,in fact,nothing more than a picturesque survival, and like most such institutions is Perhaps bound to disappear. But it will not go mninourned.

! The Registrar-General reports that the annual rate of mortality last week in the seventy-six great towns of England and Wales averaged 15.4 per 1,000. The rate in Liverpool was 20, Birkenhead 10, Wallasey 15, Bootle 20, St. Helens 16, Wigan 29, and Warrington 26. The lowest rate was the 7 of Barrow, East Ham Leyton, and Walthamstow ; and the highest York's 30. H. M. ASHTON'S Lira TONIC is the KING on' TONICS.-63, SANKEY-STREET, Warrington.—Advt. Anthrax in Cheshire. On the farm of Mrs. M. E. Gough, Aston-by-Bud- worth, two ewes were found dead on Monday, and after examination Mr. T. Eastwood, V.S., Northwich, certified an outbreak of anthrax. On Monday a cow was also cremated at Davenham. The anthrax epidemic is assuming serious proportions in Cheshire and seems to be defying all efforts to restrict it.

Warrington P.S.A. A meeting was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, on Sunday, under the presidency of Mr. Drinkwater. Mr. Hannan sang "A dream of heaven" and "Time star of Bethlehem',' and Mr. W. Hickman gave the closing address pf the year on " Stock-tak- ing." Master Pearce actdd as accompanist.

Young Men's Social. The young men of Leh-street Primitive Methodist Sunday School held their annual social on Boxing Day. A very large gathering assembled to partake of tea, after which Mr. Mairs, the teacher of the class, gave an address. Mr. Bissell also spoke. Games were indulged in, and selections on the phonograph were given by a friend.

Treat to Newsboys. The newsboys of Warrington had a high time on Tuesday afternoon when they were entertained to a capital dinner by Mr. and Mrs. Samuel Wakefield, of the Midland Hotel. This is the first time that all the newsboys of Warrington have been provided for in this manner at Christmas time and the appreciation and delight of these youthful denizens of newspaper- dom knew no bounds. As the time approached for the feast they congregated in front of the hotel and it was evident to the most casual observer that something un usual was about to take place. The newsboy lives in a world of his owni To him, personal appearance is a matter which affects him little. In the group of 200 hungry lads who assembled for the treat there were many whose unkempt and neglected appearance was a trifle forbidding but behind the rough exterior there generally beats a brave heart which helps him to overcome his trials and difficulties, and when he is the subject of a kindly act, he does not readily forget it. This week the personality of Mr. Sam Wakefield has loomed large in the newsboys' imagination and on Tuesday he was prime favourite. The limited accommoda- tion of the hotel would not permit of all the boys to sit round the festive board at one time. The youth- ful guests were therefore admitted in three batches of about seventy each, and it required all the energies of Mr. and Sirs. Wakefield and several local news- paper men to keep in order and attend to the wants of the lads. The fare consisted of roast beef, vegetables and mince pies, to which full justice was done. To complete the joy of the guests, each lad was the recipient of a threepenny piece. The lads showed their appreciation by raising three hearty cheers for the host and hostess, and the singing of" For he's a jolly good fellow."

BASSINETTES AND MAIL CARTS in latest art shades.—J. S. GARLAND, Furnishing Ironmonger, 51, 53, and 55, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Crosfield's Fire Brigade. The members of the efficient fire brigade connected 0 with Messrs.* J. Crosfield and Sons' works at Rank Quay held their first annual dinner at the Patten Arms Hotel on Saturday evening and a very enjoyable function it was. Mr. J. Gallon, the briilde captain, presided. There was a large gathering including the brigade officers, and Mr. Newall (chief engineer), Mr. Moss (shipping departmeat),Councillor Peter Peacock (traffic department), Mr. Rose (chemical department), Mr. Elliott (of Messrs. Shand and Mason), and Mr. Shaw (secretary of the North-Western district of the National- Fire Brigades' Union).—Dr. Markell was unable to be present owing to indisposition.— An excellent meal was served and after- wards various toasts were heartily honoured —Mr. Gallon proposed the health of "The Firm and its Officials." He said that as a brigade they had endeavoured to become all that the firm wished theni to become. They had, a tremendous responsibility attaching to them, and he hoped what they had already done would spur them on to better things. (Applause.) —Mr. Moss, replying for the firm and officiaN, said he was proud to belong tl the firm himself, and he might tell them that the firm were very proud of their brigade. (Applause.)—Mr. Newall proposed the health of the Fire Brigade. He mid he could say with Mr. Moss that the firm were well pleased with the brigade, but they must not rest content.—The toast was drunk with musical honours. —Mr. Elliott delivered a speech, and referred to the fact that ,the brigade had recently joined the National Fire Brigades' Union. That would help to make them' smarter. There was no doubt there were some very good fire brigades in this' cluntry, and there was always something that could be learnt. In he com- petitions they would enter, only the smartest men in the brigade would be selected to compete, and they must all strive for the honour ; but they must remem- ber that the success gained belonged to them all. Speaking of the practices at the works he said the longest time the brigade had ever taken to turn out in and get two jets on was three minutes and ten seconds. (Applause.)—The remainder of the evening Was spent in a convivial manner.

Stockton Heath Children and the Secondary School.

At a meeting of the stockton Heath Parish Council ' held on Christmas Eve, Mr. Clarke presiding, a letter was read from the Director of Education, County Education Offices, Chester, stating that he was in re- ceipt of the Parish Council's communication with re- gard to the accommodation for secondary education at Stockton Heath. He did not see any reason why pupils from Stockton Heath could. not be taken in the schools at Warrington if they presented themselves. He presumed the Council were not able to say • that such scholars had been refused.—The chairman said that scholars had been refused.—lt was decided 'that the clerk pall the attention of the Administrative Sub-committee to the matter.

St. Paul's Church House. On Monday evening a hot-pot supper was held in St. Paul's Church House, Bewsey-road, the occasion being the presentation of prizes won at the billiard handicap held in connection with the Church House over a month ago. There was a large attendance,over which the Rev. T. W. Barber presided, as the Vicar (the Rev. W. Bracecamp) had to leave early in the evening. After supper the prizes were presented by Mrs. gracecamp to the following :-1, Mr. A. H. Burgess ; 2, Thomas Spencer ; 3. W. Howie; and 4, Mr. W. Ellam. Consolation prizes : 1, James Houghton ; 2, F. P. Rath.bone ; 3, A. C. Binks. A smoking concert was afterwards held, when an enjoyable programme of vocal and instrumental music was contributed to by the Rev. C. J. Edwards and Messrs. Kendrick, Allen and Archer. The Stockton Heath Tramway.

Correspondence with reference to the Corporati tramway to Victoria Square, Stockton Heath, was submitted at a meeting of the Parish Council on Christmas Eve. Mr. Potts, clerk to the Cheshire County Council, wrote stating that he was informed. by the County Surveyor that he had had an interview with the electrical engineers and other representatives of the Corporation, with the result that it had been ,suggested that in order to get a greater width of road- way opposite the churchyard, the footpath should. be five feet wide on the churchyard side and four feet on the opposite side instead of five feet wide on each side. With reference •to 'width of the mai in Victoria-square the Corporation were prepared to carry out the requirements of the Council, which would Involve the replanting of the tree and refixing the seat round it.—To this Mr. Bradshaw replied that he had been reauested by his Council, to agree to the suggestion contained therein with regard to widths of the footpath, provided the footpath on the opposite side of the road to the church should only be reduced by such a width as would allow the requisite nine feet six inches between the kerb and the outer rail, and that in no case should the footpath be less than four feet wide.

Death of a Former Mayoress of Warrington.

Many Warrington people will regret to hear of the death of Mrs. Margaret Chrimes, relict of the late Mr. Alderman Joseph , Chrimes, Mayor of Warrington during 1855-6-7, who passed away at her residence, 24, Coventry-road, Wavertree, on Christmas Day. Mr. Chrimes died in 1869. The duties which devolved upon Mrs. Chrimes when Meyoress .of Warrington I were admirably discharged. A son was born to her Iduri.ng the first year of the Mayoralty, and Mrs. Chrimes was presented with a silver cradle and a purse of gold. For many years she kept a toy shop in Horsemarket street. Both in Warrington and Wavertree she had a large circle of friends. The present market hall was opened while Mr. Chrimes was Mayor,: and the late Sir Gilbert Greenall presented the Mayoress with a handsome I silver basket in honour of the occasion.—The funeral took place on Tuesday forenoon •at the Warrington Cemetery, the mourners present being :—Mr. Arthur Chrimes (eldest son), Eastbourne; Mrs. Chapman (daughter), Chester; Mr. and Mrs. Spncer(son-in-law and daughter), Richmond, London; Mr. Harrison Chrimes (son), Liverpool, and Mrs. Chrimes ; Mrs. Barker (sister of the deceased), Runcorn ; Mr. and Mrs. Isaac (son-in-law and daughter), Mr. Wild (South- port), Mrs. William Chrimes (daughter-in-law), Mr. Joseph Chrimes (grandson), Mr. Pilling, Mr. Wood, and Mr. C. Tomkinson (Liverpool), Mr. Peter Chrimes (Bridge-street), and Mr. Ephraim Aukland Froghall- lane. The Rector of Warrington officiated. The deceased was 75 years of age, and the cause of death was bronchitis.

A Roumanian's Donation to the Infirmary.

The Infirmary Secretary (Mr. J. HP. Hamps-on) has received an unexpected but none the less welcome donation to the funds of the institution this week. On Tuesday, Pr. Oscar Radvaner, 'who fora wager of £lO,OOO offered by the Jockey Club of Bucharest is engaged in a walk round the world, revisited this town and calling at the Infirmary, left .a contri- bution card in his father's name for .£25, to be honoured at a well-known bank. Dr. Radvaner has . undertaken the task of visit- ing every country in the world in eight years, one of which is allowed for possible illness. He has to walk th,e whole of the journey except where it is absolutely impossible to do so. He is forbidden to use his private means, a ticket from the .Jockey Club enabling him to travel by sea and to obtain food along the route. He left Bucharest on July 16th, 1900,being thell 18 years of age, and proceeded to Russia, where he had an interview with the Czar, and received a passport to travel through Siberia and a present of 1,000 roubles. In Siberia he experienced great hard- ships. He had to swim the river Lenat part of the contract being that he must swim all unbridged rivers less than 60 yards wide. He wandered for -five days in the Himalayan Mountains having lass his compass. He then travelled through • India, Afghanistan, Asia Minor, and by way of Constantinople through Bulgaria, where he was seized by gendarmes, but afterwards liberated. In Macedonia he was captured by insurgents and relieved of all his valuables, only being released when the nature of his task was made known. He crossed the Balkans, went through-Servia and Hungary, had an interview with the Emperor Francis Joseph, and on reaching Berlin received a great reception from 3,000 students. On arriving in England he was received by the King, who arranged for a passport for the Transvaal. Dr. Radvaner on leaving• England, will travel through South Africa, France, Switzerland and Italy. It is, he states, his intention, if he wins the wager, to build an Infirmary at Bucharest.

Death of Mr. Joseph Faulkner. We regret to record the somewhat sudden death of Mr. Joseph Faulkner who for about ten years had held the position of booking clerk at the Central Station. On Monday morning last week Mr. Faulkner who was 30 years of age, and resided at 42, Bewsey- street, was apparently in his usual health and intended to go away for a day's holiday. He went home to make preparations, when he suddenly became ill. A. few hours later his condition became so serious that it was found necessary to call medical assistance. Dr.. Fox, and Dr. Fox, jun.. attended him, but Mr. Faulkner gradually sank and 'died on Saturday night, the cause of death being pneumonia. A sympathetic reference was made to the deceased by the Vicar of St. Pauls in his sermon on Sunday evening. Mr. Faulkner was a member of the Warrington Cycling Club and the Conservative Club. The funeral took place on Wednesday. The cortege left Bewsey-road in the morning and proceeded by road to .deceased!s home at Tarvin. The Vicar of Tarvin (the Rai,. T. O. Evans) conducted a short service in . the house, and afterwards the procession wended its way to Ashton Hayes, where the interment took place in the family grave in the churchyard. The Vicar of Ash-. ton Hayes officiated •in the - Church and at the graveside. • The - following meMbers Of the Cheshire Lines- staff at Warrington acted as bearers :—Me'sSrs. H. Holloway, H. A. Roberts, W. li. Smith, E. Hughes, J. Dutton and H. T. Williams. The chief mourners were :—Mr. J. •Faulkner (father), Mr. and Mrs.. J. Faulkner, jun., land Mr. and Mrs. S. Faulkner (brothers and sisters- in-law), Mr. and Mrs. Nixon (sister and brother-in- law), Misses L. and M. Faulkner (sisters), and Mr. F. Nixon, Mr. .J. W. Davies, chief clerk at the Central Station booking office under whose directions the deceased had worked for the past ten years, was also present. The Warrington Conserva- tive Club was represented by Councillor Wilson, Messrs. 'Shacklady, Crosby and E. Morris. Many beautiful floral tributes were forwarded by Warring- ton friends, including Mr. and Mrs. Astbury (with whom the late Mr. Faulkner had resided ever since lie came to Warrington), the members of the Con- servative and Cycling Clubs, and the stationmaster and staff at the Central Station.

BEST BOOTS FOR ALL.—UNDER the CLOCK.—Advt.

A Christmas Day Fatality. The circumstances attending the death of Charles Ganderton, 87 years-of age, who resided with his son- in-law, William Cleaton, off-licence holder, Bewsey- road, were inquired into by Coroner Brighouse and a jury at the Police Station on Monday afternoon.— The evidence showed that on Christmas night decased left the kitchen at 10.40 to go to bed. Shortly after- wards Mr.. Cleaton heard a thud at the bottom of the stairs and on going there found deceased lying on his face. He was bleeding from a wound on the head and was unconscious.—Mr. Cleaton said deceased had been in the. habit of having his collar unfastened by some member of the family before going to bed. On the night in question he left the kitchen before this had been done, and Mr. Cleaton was of the opinion that he was going downstairs again for that purpose, when he overbalanced himself and fell.—The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death." Runcorn Rural District Coun—cil decided on. Tues- day to call the attention of the Manchester Ship Canal Company to the delays occasioned by the swing bridge at Stockton Heath being kept open for an un- Idue length of time to enable vessels to pass. The matter came up for consideration on a recommenda- tion of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee . that the company should be prosecuted Mr. Clarke introduced the discussion .at the committee meeting and cited two cases, one delay, it was alleged,atuounting to 23 mintites.TheAct provided that the bridge should be kept open only 10 minutes. at a time. Delays were repeatedly occuring, and as the traffic of the district was increasing to a great extent, the inconvenience would become pro- nortionately greater. He moved a resolution to the effect that the District Council be asked to take up the cases and that the Ship Canal Company be summoned before the Magistrates at, DaresburyrettySeisions.— Mr. Whitley seconded. Mr. Bouchier moved an amendment that the attention of the. District Gloun- cil be called to the delay in the opening and closing of the bridge, and it should be understood the committee felt it was caused chiefly in the early morning and at right owing to the want of extra hands. The Ship Canal Company should be asked to provide extra assistance.—There was, however, no seconder and the amendment fell to the ground, Mr. Clarke's resolution being carried. A Tanners' Labourer's Sad End. An inquest was conducted at the Police Station on Monday afternoon by Mr. S. Brighouse, touching the death of William Ravenscroft, aged 66, a tanners' labourer residing at 47, Fairclough's-avenue, who was found dead in the engine house at the Vernon Tanning Company's works on Saturday morning.—Joseph Forster, the manager for the Company and son-in-law of the deceased, said he last saw Ravenscroft alive on . December 19th, When he appeared to be in his usual health. At nine o'clock on Saturday morning witness went to the tannery, and on going into the engine house found deceased lying there as if asleep. He tried to rouse him but failed, and on examination found him to be dead. For'some time past deceased had been drink- ing heavily and ,had neglected himself in every way.— Emily Daniels, wife of Charles John Daniels, with whom the deceased lodged said that at 6.30 on Wed- nesday night deceased left the house stating that he was goin4 to pawn a pair of shoes in order to obtain more drink.—Dr. Smith said he had made an examination of the body and there was nothing to sug- gest that deceased had died from •violence. Witness was of the opinion that a stroke of apoplexy was the\l cause of death.—A verdict of "death from naturall causes" was returned.

WATERS.—Advt.

Death of a Warrington Police Officer.

On Christmas Eve, Sergeant Lawrence, the Police Officer who had been specially employed at Messrs. Pearson and Knowles' Works, I:lied at his residence in Willis-street. Sergeant Lawrence joined the War- rington Borough Police 'in -1877,, when the forae was just half its present strength, and for the last 18 years had done duty at the Bewsey and Dallam Works, for the greater part of the time holding the rank of sergeant. He had been in failing health for some considerable time and was off duty for about three months previous to his death which was due to an affection of the heart. Deceased was 58 years of age and leaves a widow and a grown up family of two sons and two daughters. As a! courteous Police Officer and alhighly intelligent man, Sergeant Lawrence was held in much respect by thosel who knew him. The funeral took place on Sunday, when a detachment of the County Borough Police,. in charge of Chief Inspector Phillips, walked in, front of the hearse from deceased's residence to the Cemetery, whilst Inspectors Taylor and Rowbotham, Sergeants Cheney and Smith and Constables Hardy and Corbett acted as bearers. Mr. J. Price of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, along with several of the em- ployees of the Company met the cortege at the Cemetery. There was a number of wreaths on the coffin amongst which was a large one, in the form of a Maltese cross, from the members of the Police Force.

; NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS, ' THE LABOUR REPRESENTA- Rugby Football. TION QUESTION. By " Ovalist." Saturday's performance stamps Warrington as being-- a better team than most people imagined. They SOUTH AFRICA'S YELLOW PERIL. defeated Salford at the Willows in no half-hearted , fashion and caused consternation in the camp of the Reds. It was probably the very last Christmas ,box the Salford men expected, though Bradford opened their eyes somewhat the previous day. A loss of four points in the two engacteinents sadly spoiled Salford's League record, but other prominent clubs went under similarly and things are pretty much as before in the top storey. Still it will take the Salford crowd a good while to gt over the shock of defeat by Warrington on the sacred Weaste enclosure

Somehow there was a feeling abroad that Warring- ton had -a better chance than usual on this occasion. They had handsomely accounted for Runcorn a week before, and it was fancied that the Reds would not be quite so fresh as usual after the previous day's encounter with Bradford. It must have been" the hope that lives eternal" etc., that induced the thousands of Warringtonians to make do journey to the Royal Borough. " They:came not empty away," either, and while there they made their presence felt unmistakeably on several occasions, especially when Fish capped his previous performances by running the length of the field to score. The cheer then was simply deafening, and many hundred Salfordians joined in, so in- fectious was the exhilaration of the moment. Of course, these were not the most rabid partizans. The crowd was unquestionably a sportsmanlike one and recognised merit when it saw the same. You could not always say the same at New Barns.

Warringtor.'s last victory over Salford away was on the New Barnes ground—and it is a few years ago now. Fair Barber was the hero of the occasion. He scored a goal and a try in the first minute. I remem- ber it distinctly. " Smack " Bate got the ball from the scrum and Barber made one of those flying dashes which occasionally came off. He received at the pre- cise moment and was clear of the home backs in a twinkling. Mainivaring, the full back, never had a chance, and Barber sped along through a blizzard of hail and rain until he had groundeciounder the bar. Salford never recwered froth the blow and Warring- ton came away conquerors.

The match on Saturday was quite different, all the scoring being done in the last quarter. Up to half-time there had not been a pin to choose between the teams Though Salford had a considerable pull in the scrum- mage, Warrington neutralised the effect by superior play behind. Lomas and his men were not allowed much latitude and when the interval arrived there was no score. The second half saw Warrington making some headway. The men were lasting better than their opponests and the ball came out oftener on the Warrington side. While defending Dan Smith made a fatal mistake. He failed to hold the ball, and Davies coming up rapidly, robbed him and scored under the posts, Fish adding a goal. The Warring- ton suppprters were in high spirits until the Reds forced the game at the other end. Several frantic efforts by Lomas failed but before Warrington could clear Bedford got over at the corner. The place was too far out for Lomas to land a goal and Warrington resumed from the centre with a two points lead.

Salford were now spent, and Warrington overplayed them at all points. A good position was soon obtained in the home quarters, and brilliant passing by Bate, Davies, Isherwood and Fish resulted in the latter dashing in at the corner. There was no goal. Salford made a fine rally, and Lomas ran down to the. War- rington end where he was well tackled. At the same instant Fish snapped up the ball and was off like a flash. The Reds tried hard to head him off,but. Jack gained at every stride and finally completed a brilliant individual effort with a third try. Warrington attack- ed until the close and made their superiority unques- tionable. Eleven points to three against Salford is one of the best things the teim has done for a long time.

The .Warrington men, generally, played well. Hallam was not quite at his best, and Bate did not' reach his previous week's standard, but the others all performed splendidly. Davies worked hard against his old clubmates and it must' have been a bitter pill to them when he opened the scoring. It was at three- quarter where Warrington were markedly superior. The combination on. the attack was unsatisfactory until the close, but individually the quartette were more than a match for their opponents, who • handled the ball at least twice as often. A word of praise is due to the Warrington forwards, who set a hot pace and kept it up. Thomas played a wonderful game and repeatedly earned cheers for line football. On the Salford side L3mas was the star, as usual, but the support he got was not by any. means great. Smith was distinctly rocky in the rear, while the rest of the hacks were mediocre. The forwards worked hard,but the strain of two successive matches told on them towards the close. s

Since Marsden's injury the Bradford Club have been on the look out for a smart half-back, and have secured a Scotch player of some note. His name is Will Ingles the well known half-back of the Hawick Club. hides, was recently chosen in the South of Scotland team in the trial match, but he could not turn out owing to an injured ankle. He is 22 years of age of sturdy build, and he comes to Bradford with the ' very highest credentials.

[By. Mn. HENRY Were we inclined to let loose, and pour out all that the lumber room of memory has stored up touching the "Peace with Honour" episode of years ago what a deal Might be said to prove the soundness of the Grand Old Man's position on the Majuba Hill dis- aster. Subsequent events, with a climax that has re- suited in a long total of deaths and debts, with such lan exposure of govennental incapability such as the modern world has never before seen should surely- ' open the eyes of the British workman to the neces- sity, of bestiring himself and determining once for all that the composition of the House of Commons should and must be made up more iin conformity with twentieth century ideas. The most gratifying feature in Warrington's politica! ; history that I have seen for some time is the one have just discovered through the " Examiner." Warrington has suddenly stumbled across the Labour Representation' question. Long years of experiencd and study of labour questions has con- vinced me of two things. First, that county members, who 'are` as a rule Tory, have neither the inclination nor .yet the mental capacity to judge fairly and dis- passionately the ever increasing number of questions that affect British commerce and touch to the quick the life of the British workman. And secondly, the ! urgent need there is to send to the H-use of Commons men who by their life's records have proved that their capability, and honesty of purpose fit them especially to leErislate for their fellow workers. A deal might be said on this question just now, but 1 must limit myself to a very few words of advice. Stand by the Liberal party. Its record is not all one would wish, as witness the Bradlaugh tragedy. Yet it can and must be made all the workers want and wish for. The creation of non-conformist parties and Independent Labour parties are but red herrings drawn across the workman's Path, and must surely lead him away from his quarry. I want to speak of South Africa, for I have just read that the employment .of Asiatic labour in that said-to- be-conquered country is assured. There are some questions of which a man may speak with authority, and I think I fill the order this time, if you allow the Asiatics and by the term, I mean the Chinese,Japanese and Corean, to have free access to South Africa you will surely, surely, ah ! as surely as death find, within a very few years that you have run up against the toughest probre-mi a nation, a civilized nation, ever had to solve. "2:Understand, almost every city, town and village in the United States has its China town, but the China town San Francisco, as centred in its very heart is the boss of. them all. It has been my lot on several occasions, once officially, to see our pest hole in San Francisco from the inside, and what I have seen I could not possibly whisper through the columns of the "Examiner." The China- man is a barbarian, the Jap and his brother, the Corean, a little less so. The two latter must be looked at from a labour point of view. The former must be looked at all round. Yet these people,—l suppose we should call ' even Hottentots "people,"—have friends, and you may hear some of your candidates, who have travelled a little and seen the surface of things,repeat the old jargon, "Is he not a man and a brother? I say distinctly "He is not a man," and most assuredly he is a brother, nor would any one of those who are anxious for him to become a British subject, or live as an alien under -the British flag, hurry up to hold out the glad hand to 'him even as a brother, or asa son-in-law. _

Do you want a race of people who do not, nay, who cannot assimilate, to increase and multiply under the Uritish flag? Do you want this people simply because their labour is cheap,and you must have the gold? Do you want them because they are obsequious, and are always neatly dressetrin white when waiting at the traveller's table? If you do,you will surely have, along with the cheap labour and an increased output of gold along with the apparent cleanliness,and cringing civility a few other features. You will certainly find that for ways ;that are dark, and for tricks that are vain, the heathen Chinee is peculiar. He can smile child-like and bland, but follow him when he leaves your presence and seeks the company of his own, and then say if you want him as an associate of the white man. It is a fact, with abundance of proof, that the Chinee drags down to the pit of hell in which he lives the white man or woman with whom he conies in daily con- tact. It may be asked how it comes about that a China-town can continue to exist in a city like San Francisco? How is it, that it is not cleared out bag and baggage? The answer is summed up in one word,— 'corruption.' Wea,ith runs the city, and inasmuch as no white man, with any respect for himself will live iu China-town, so will no white man take a house a Chinee has lived in. It, follows that wealthy owners of the property have a "vested interest" in the continu- ance of the curse.

It is said the Chinee is one of "God's creatures." Well, there is no accounting for tastes,he is not one of mine. Let me give a few samples of Ah Sin's behaviour as a laundry man. With a smile that is pensive and childlike he attends the Christian church, and Brooklyn, New York, is aroused jus t now with the knowledge of such fact. The yellow brutes have been enticing young girls to their dens, and alnumber of these laundrymen have been arrested. Re- volting as are the crimes with which these Asiatics stand charged, they seem to have established an un- shakable hold upon the sympathy, not to say the affec- tion, of a lot of white women. These latter have been carrying flowers to the interesting prisoners in their cells, weeping over them, and declaring them to be persecuted victims—not innocent, it may be, but more sinned against than sinning. It would be ver3, difficult to state, with due regard for decency, the plea that some of the females make for their 'pig-tailed friends. And the strange part of it is that these women, so lost to pity for the.children of their sex who have been namelessly misused, are teachers in Sunday schools and call themselves missionaries. Thelailed Chinese have been pupils in their;classes, Where instruction in Chris- tian doctrine and morals is supposed to •be imparted. Incapable, apparently, of blushing for themselves, the police have blushed for them, and used as strong meas- ures as permissible to keep them and their flowers and tears away from the cells. The magistrate in this case spoke out with the vehemence of a just indignation as to the conduct of these women._ _ _

_ It is to be kept steadily in mind by all who have to do with the Chinaman that he is a barbarian, with the modes of thought, the habits and vices of his kind. It is the testimony of all missionaries, not neurotic and immodest women playing at being such, that the effort to Christianize the Chinese is among the heaviest of the white man's burdens. He simply becomes a convert for what there is in it, business. He has no initiation, but is good at imitation. He will take up cobbling, wash- ing, laundry work and cigar making. These lines of business he will carry on in dens fairly reeking with filth. In these dens, in which he eats, sleeps and passes his:strange life he does more strange andi outrageous things than are wot of by the mere sight seer. To invite the white boy, girl, man, cr woman to these places with the tempting and seducing pleasures of opium and morphine is to him a great achievement. The law may step in and make a pretence of stopping all this, but the law is not Argus eyed. When a stranger determined to see the sights of !Chinatown, seeks a guide, he pays his dollar but he does not see China- town, and yet the reeking abominations. are to be seen in open 'day, without descending and wandering through the warrens, that honey- comb, bar the sweetness, and undermine the whole of the pig-tail region. Until just recently one could wander through the various alleys and see in the grated windows of the hovels on each side, the painted faces of Chinese girls, mere children, effering their bodies for sale, and reciting an unfit-for-publication, shibboleth to man and boy alike. This traffic prospered to such an extent that the medical fraternity had to in- terpose in defence, or on behalf of tlw health, of the youth, of the city. Only law, and never the moral ebli- gation and responsibility can stay the Chinaman from the pursuit of the habits and vices of his nature. So much for the immoral side of the Asiatic South African question. It has a near relationship to the state into which the labour question is drifting. If you allow the Asiatic cookie to-gain a firm footing, and be sure his footing will be firm if he plants that foot at all in South Africa, then get ready for the burning question of the future the like of which the British Government never yet had to face. Lents have an ob- ject lesson right here, it may help the workingman to decide to whom he will give his vote at the coming election.

It may be known to a few of ray readers that one of the results of the Spanish-American war has been an extension of American territory, notably the Philippine Islands, and Hawaiian Islands, At ,the latter place, T may say, my son now resides, and these places are now being used as a bridge over which coolie labour, cheap labour, is now trying to pass into California. in the Hawaiian islands, out of a population of 160,000, there arel7o,ooo Japanese, 25,000 Chinese,and of late,Coreans, who are Chinese in build and customs, are brought in by the Sugar Planters Association. This employing body intend to bring in 10,000 aS an experiment. Their transference to the Pacific Coast will not be a hard matter. The Japanese are now driving to destitution the native Hawaiians, the few white merchants, and the few white mechanics. Take my word for it, they will do the same thing in South Africa. The British Govern- ment is now leading the working voters away from the real burning questions that are at issue. in Japan now wages of labourers are 9d. a day, and of mechanics from 10d: to 2s. a day, and it is this class of people the British Government now propose to place in competi- tion with the native and the whites in opposition t,) the wishes of the leading Boers. General Botha has written to the newspaprrs of Pretoria, South Africa, warning his fellow Boers against those who, he says are unlaw- fully using his name to convene meetings in support of

r the "introduction of Chinese labour, and thus entrap- ping Boers into passing resolutions in favour of tie. importation of Chinese. General Botha says that such agents are not acting in the interests of the Boers but against them. The question, he continues, is perhaps the most serious one that South Africa has ever had to face,and is, perhaps, fraught with the greatest disaster. The British voter ha 4 now something else to think of if he wishes his country to hold her place among the nations, as of old, than the sendinr, to Parliament of a man merely pledged to support the Nonconformists' plan of campaign. He must send men who will clear out of the way that huge monstrosity, the established Church. No established church and there would be no ;fooling ' with the educating of the growing British citizen. No established church and there would beno land question out of which Lord Penrhyn could force a large body of men to be idle for long months,, because he, forsooth, was thej owner of slate quarries which the Almighty is supposed to have been busy creating millions of years ago. Men . who would support the removal of problems like these would also support, and with earne4ness, Mr. Bell in his struggle with the Taff Vale decision.

The above aro a few of the burning questions which should engage the attention of every wage earner. They should be well ventilated in the labour organiza- tion and the candidate who knows nothing of them, and who has not already decided on which side the edge he stands should receive his quietus, belie known either as Liberal. Or Towv. San Francisco.

HENRY WHITHAAL

The Street Improvement Committee has resolved that the Electricity and Tramways Committee be requested to prepare plans showing the through tramway routes in the four, principal streets, and the turnings and crossings at Market Gate, so as to enable this com- mittee to prepare plans for the proposed widening of those streets.

The Town Clerk reported that the sum to be paid to Mr. R. E. Garnett for the land required for the new street was £1,154, and the sum to be paid by Mr. Garnett to the Corporation for the land to be taken by him £5OO, leaving the net cost to the Corporation of £645, plus the ground rent of £lO per annum. As against this the Corporation will have available for re- sale 182 square yards of land which would have been absorbed into the proposed passage. SANITARY WORKS APPOINTMENTS.

The S-mitary Works Committee has resolved, that Mr. J. J. A. Parfitt be appointed the Manager of the Cleansing Department, at a salary of £l5O per annum, rising by £lO per annum to a maximum of £200; and that Mr. Richard Wilson be appointed the Manager of the Longford Depot, at a salary of £l5O per annum, with annual increases of £lO to a maximum of £2OO. THE BRANCEI LIBRARY QUESTION. The Museum Committee having considered the ques- tion of the desirability of establishing branch libraries to report that a branch library might take any of the following forms_:— _ _

(iy An independent lending library with separate staff and stock of books, and with a newsroom attached. (2) A newsroom with small reference library, and with or without a boys' room. (3) A delivery station, with small stock of books on deposit. (4) A school issue centre, for the use of scholars. *only. Having regard to the extent and distribution of the population of the borough, and to the question of ex: pense, the committee do not suggest at present the formation of any independent branches, but consider that it is desirable to establish one or more delivery stations. These would be established, by preference in shops, the shopkeeper acting as agent, and being paid a certain sum per week for accommodation and attend- ance. A small selection of books would be deposited at. each station, and in addition -any book might be requisitioned by a borrower from the Central Library and delivered to him at the station. The cost of such stations would depend partly upon the frequency with which books were exchanged between the Branch and -the Library, it may be roughly eatimated at £2O a year for each station. It is not anticipated that any capital expendi- ture would be required. The Committee recommend that as an experiment not more than five delivery stations be opened. The Committee further propose to take steps to form issue centreslin Elementary Schools with the help and under the control of the head teachers These centres would be supplied with books from a special stock kept for the purpose. For the mainten ance of the delivery stations and school centres, the Committee recommend that a sum of £l5O be included in theestimates for 1904-5 in addition to the usual Aluseum grant. The Committee further recommend as an experiment that an evening reading-room be opened at the Silver-street Schools provided the cost does not exceed £52 a year, and request that a further grant of £52 be included in the estimates for 1904-5 to meet the cost thereof.

CORONER'S CRITICISM OF A JURY'S VERDICT.

On Tuesday afternoon an adjourned inquiry was held at St. Helens into the death of Joseph Buckley, a waggon trimmer, who died on the 17th inst. from in juries received the previous day by being jammed be tween two coal waggons at the Ashton's Green Colliery A question had arisen whether the waggons had jumped a 4in. scotch, and the inquiry had been adjourned to enable the jury to witness some experiments at the coliery. Dr. Jackson, who had madea post-mortem examination, said that from the nature of the injuries received Buckley had evidently been crushed between the buffers of the waggons. The jury found that Buckley had died from injuries, but they were not able to agree that the injuries had been received in the manner described by the evidence. It was not possible for a waggon to jump a 4in. scoWh. The Coroner vointed out that Buckley had said before he died that he was jammed by the waggons, and that the scotch slipped. However, the jury had returned their verdict, and the police had better inquire into this and see if they could bring anybody Up for murder or manslaughter. Their verdict meant that they did not think it was an accident, and that someone/was to blame. It was an unfortunate verdict to record. and if every verdict was to follow the evidence in the same erratic way there would be very little confidence in the verdict' of coroner's juries. The fact was that the majority of the jury had been dominated by one who hada stronger will than the others. The Foreman said that that was a very unfair observation, and a reflection on the jury.

THE STOMACH'S DAY'S WORK.

If any man or woman had to work as hard at the Christmas and New Year holiday period as his or her own stomach has to work, there would be a strike. Little wonder then that the stomach occasionally gets wrong, and most people at this time of the year find it necessary to take a few of Chas. Forde's bile beans to keep this busy organ up to par. This is , a list of what the stomach has to do every day : Tarn several pounds of solid food into a semi- liquid state. Digest that amount, turning it into a form from which it may be made into bone. muscle, or other tissue. Make its wan supply of gastric juice to digest with, 10 to 20 pints per day. Keep up a heat of 100 degrees, notwithstanding all the cold water, ice cream, etc., that is taken. If the stomach gets below that temperature, if it ceases to secrete gastric juice, food decomposes, wind collects, flatulence and pain follow. There is loss of appetite, headache, sometimes constipation, 44,nd other symptoms of digestive disorder. Every day makes it harder for the hard-worked stomach to get back to its proper state, until unless helped it breaks down completely. Chas. Forde's bile beans help in a purely natural manner. They clean away, decomposing matter, gently open the bowels, tone up the secreting glands, and in other ways are most beneficial. Mr. Andrew Kerr, of Great Bedwyn Manor, Wilts., says —"I: had indigestion for nearly a year, and my condition was becoming most serious when I tried bile beans. They cured me pompletely,and since then I have not had the faintest trace of indigestion." 'this great vegetable remedy is obtainable from all chemists and medicine vendors. Avoid substitutes.

WARRINGTON CONSERVATIVE CLUB Annual Meeting.

The annual meeting of the members of the Warrington Conservative Club was held on Wednesday evening under the presidency of Alderman Francomb. A good attendance included Alderman Sutton, Councillors John Roberts and Wilson, Messrs. G. Pilling (hon. secretary), W. Owen (treasurer), E. Draper W. W. Westwell, C. Leigh, W. H. Bellian, R. Daintith, J. Thornton, W. Hol- brook (Conservative agent), and others. Mr. Pru.ING presented the annual report which stated that the condition and prospects of the club were satis- factory. Although there was a slight falling-off in membership, the committee believed it to be of a temporary nature, and they were of the opinion that there were many in the town and district who, if approached, would become members. The bowling green had during the season been inexcellent order and considering the great amount of rain which must liave stopped play, it had been well attended and proved suc- cessful. The usual mains for members took place at the Opening and close of the season. The annual billiard handicap was again held, and a Large number of mem- bers took part. The resignation of Mr. and Mrs. Wat- kins as manager and manageress of the club, after hold- ing the offices nearly ten years, was accepted by the Committee, and in response to advertisements for their successors no fewer than 300 applications were received. This caused a very large amount of work to be gone through in making the selection, and ultimately Mr. and Mrs. Sangster, of Manchester, were appointed. The Committee believed they had every reason to congratu- late themselves upon the appointment. Councillor JOHN ROBERTS moved the adoption of the report and paid a tiibnte to the ability with which Mr. and Mrs. Sangster had discharged their duties. It was a matter for satisfaction that the club was in a good financial potition. Mr. McDowni, seconded and the report was adopted. Mr. OWEN reported that during the year the club had been as successful as ever. There was a balance in hand and they had made money during the year. The heaviest expenditure had been oa the billiard tables and he thought the members would approve of it. He had only one wish in regard to the club, and that was that the membership might be increased. On the motion of Mr. C. LEIGH seconded by 3lr. WESTWELL the treasurer's statement was adopted. The CHAIRUAN then addressed the meeting and said the Committee were satisfied that the club was in a. very good state financially,and everything seemed to be prospering. Year by year he told them the same tale— that they did not encourage gambling, and he was proud to say that the members were more determined than ever that that sort of thing should be put a stop to. If there was the slightest appearance of any gambling games being introduced they were at once reported and put down with a very strong hand. They, however, encouraged bowls and billiards because they were harm- less games of skill and afforded the members a great deal of recreation. During the year they had lost the services of Mr. and Mrs. Watkins who had been with them nearly ten years and resigned in order to improve their position. It was the wish of every member of the club that they might be very successful in their new undertaking. They had obtained the services of Mr. and Mts. Sangster, who during the two months they had been at the club had performed their duties in a manner that would be impossible to excel. In conclusion ho wished all the members a happy new year and hoped the coming year would be as successful as the one which had just closed. (Applause).

• The committee was then re-elected with the substi- tution of Mr. J. Thornton for the late Mr. JACox. The committee is constituted as follows :—Alderman Francomb, Councillors Roberts and Wilson, Messrs W. Owen, George Pilling, George Jolley, D. Cooper, R. Chadwick, V. IL Bellian, J. Thornton, Wi W. Westwell, C. Leigh, C. G. Mc Dowel, and J. Strettle and Alderman Smethurst, Mr. William Silcock and Mr. T. Mason (appointed by the association). Mr. G. PILLING was re-elected hon. secretary and Mr. N. Owen hon. treasurer, and a vote of thanks was accorded to them for their past services. Mr. Robert Daintith was also thanked for his assistance. On the motion of the CHAIRMAN, 'seconded by Coun- cillor Wilson, the meeting passed a vote of condolence with the relatives of the late Mr. Joseph Faulkner who haa been a member of the club for ten years. A vote of thanks was accorded to the CHAIRMAN who in reply, alluded to his long association with the club. He was, he said, for a long time chairman of the old club in Academy-street, and when the present building was opened Captain Reynolds was elected to the chaulnanstup. On his death he (Alderman Fran- comb) again took up the office and had held it ever since.

FALL OF A TOWN CLOCK HAADIER.

Whilst striking the hour of 11 on Wedneiday morn- ing, the huge hammer of the Bangor town clock, perched on a tower 60 feet above the highest house, broke from its fastening, and, with a shower of bolts and nuts, fell into the street. The hammer just missed the head of a, cabman, and deeply indented a concrete slab. For- tunately none of the flying nuts struck anybody. The clock was presented to the town 20 years ago by Alder- man Thomas Lewis, in commemoration of his mayoralty.

The motion was then put to the meeting an heartily agreed to. Mr. LOWE, in reply, said it was no use finding out all these facts if they were not to be made use of. With reference to precautionary measures, of course mosquito nets had been known long enough. One great means of doing sway with the disease was to take the creature in its earlier stages, as it had been done with great success in Sierra Leone and Egypt. Paraffin oil. when poured on the pools destroyed the cause of the disease. The dangerous mosquito was generally found in puddles and in marshy districts. The mosquito was not a big traveller. It was so frail that it seldom got away. It had never been known to fly more than half a mile away from the place where it was Latched. There- fore, if they wished to be free from malaria they must do away with the deadly swamps, puddles and swampy ditches. With reference to Mr. (keening, he said he did not mention his name and what he owed to him, because no words of his could do justice to the subject. Malaria was not a new disease, but it had not appeared in England to any extent during the past two or three generations. They had lost sight of it, but it was frightfully common in the tropics. We had better sanitation now, and our houses were kept in a better sanitary condition. We had got rid entirely of malarial fever in England, and he did not think they were likely to have it again. The last known case was in 1897, in Norfolk, where there was an undoubted case of malaria - With reference to the question as to whether living in. a three-storey building would enable anyone to escape the mosquito, Mr. Lowe pointed out that a mosquito always preferred to devote itself to the lower parts of a house, although he did not think one would be abso- lutely safe even in a third storey. Quinine destroyed the parasite in the blood, and salt of arsenic had also been found useful.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

SUDDEN DEATH.

On Sunday night. Mrs. Sarah Anne Sefton, a widow aged sixty years, of Windmill-street, Runcorn died very suddenly. During the afternoon Mrs. Sefton, accom- panied by her daughter, paid some visits, and retur n- inghome, she lay on the sofa to rest. Afterwards sh e arose, walked across the room, sat down in a chair and. immediately expired.

A DANGEROUS WEAPON.

A smart yowlg foreign sailor named Hermann Steen- kemp was brought up at Runcorn, on Monday, charged with being drunk and disorderly. Superintendent Ennion said that in the prisoner's possession he found a "knuckle-duster" of the worst pattern made. It would ruin anyone struck on the face with it.—The magis- trates asked the prisoner what he was doing with such a weapon, and he replied that he got it on Sunday night somewhere, but he did not know whore :—A fine of 10s. and costs was imposed. RUNCORN FOOTBALL CLUB'S TROUBLES,

It has just transpired that most of the players of Runcorn F.C. threatened not to journey to Salford on Monday if they were not paid the same rate as for a Yorkshire match. The committee .refused to comply with the demand, and nine members of the A team were called upon. Eventually the team,with two exceptions, Woods and Moulton, went to Salford, and lost easily. The A team players were recompensed with ss. each, although they did not play. The committee have since had a meetink and interviewed Captain Butterworth,and Woodshas been suspended for two months and Moulton for a fortnight. Harry Price (centre three-quarters) has announced his intention not to play for the club again. His reasons are not monetary which is only a secondary consideration to him,and the committee have appointed a deputation to wait upon him and persuade him to withdraw his decision. His withdrawal would be a great loss to the club.

HOW COOLNESS HAS AVERT ED

GREAT DANGERS.

Great presence of mind in cases of emergency, where lives lihng upon the thought and action-of seconds,is so rare as to excite universal admiration. 4A party of Rimington's Scouts were galloping back to their column hotly pursued by a-much superior force of Boers,during the recent South African AV ar. Suddenly they came upogi a still fence of barbed wire. They had no wire cutter amour, them, so turned at right angles, and galloped along it, looking for an opening. Instead, they found another fence running at right angles to the first. They were hopelessly trapped. A trooper named Fraser suddenly extricated himself from the throng. He pulled his feet from his stirrups, rammed home his spurs and went at the fence as hard as he could pelt. The shock was fearful. The horse was killed on the spot, the man hurled many yards over its head. But the wires were snapped, and the others riding through the gap, picked up the insensible body ' of their comrade and escaped. An unknown number of people owe theirlives to John Philip Sousa. On one occasion he was playing to. an audience of 12,000 people at St. Louis, when the electric lights in the hall suddenly went out. Some one shouted " Fire !" and an ominous rustle made itself heard through the gloom. Rap, rap, went Sousa's baton, and without an instant's hesitation the band burst forth into "Oh, dear, what ban the matter be?" The uneasy rustle turned to a 'ripple of laughter, and when this air was rapidly followed by "Wait Till the Clouds Roll By" a roar of merriment showed that the situation was saved. By -just such quick thought, a keeper in a Hamburg menagerie saved a comrade's life which was apparently beyond all human aid. A full-grown tiger had suddenly turned upon the cleaner who had entered its cage. It was between him and the door, creeping slowly upon him with stealthy steps and 'yellow, glowing eyes. The man, paralysed with terror, stood motion- less against the bars. There was only one spectator of the scene, and he was • unarmed. But quick wit provided him with a better weapon than I hot irons or.rifles. A sharp hiss pierced the stillness, just the sound that th e great python of the Indian jungle makes when coiled ready to strike. The tiger heard it, and its body quivered, and seemed to grow smaller. Another hiss, and the savage brute sank down cowering upon the floor of its cage, and thought no more of the terrified man, who lost no time in escaping.

It may be remembered 'that the transport " Rapiilari" was forced by fire to return to Birkenhead after start- ing with troops and horses for South Africa. That the whole vessel, her cargo, and the lives of all her crew and passengers were not lost is due to the presence of mind and magnificent courage of one man. The fire first broke out among some stores near the engine room and before anything could be done a couple of great drums of paraffin had burst, enveloping the whole engine room in a roaring sheet of flame. Hose could not be brought to bear and the ship's doom appeared to be sealed. Suddenly the beat of the pistons ceased, and a welcome sound of escaping steam was heard. Rushing about in the furnace beneath, the chief engineer had first stopped the engines and then turned on all the steam cocks and drains. So quick was he that he escaped without being severely burned. The hatches were closed down, and the fire was put our almost as rapidly as it had begun. A recent chief official of one of His Majesty's prisons had a well-deserved reputation for extraordinary cool- ness and quickness in time of danger. At one time he was chief warder, and was sitting alone in his office *hen a slight sound behind made him turn round. A dangerous convict was creeping upon him, armed with an iron bar. "Don't you move !" hissed the criminal, "I'm going to get out if I have to kill you !" "Oh," said the official, "I thought it wits to-morrow you were going." The man looked at him in stupid amazement. "Yes," said the official, "didn't you know? A pardon came for you to-day in consideration of your good conduct. You can go now, I suppose, if you want to. You'd like to see the papers. They're in here, I believe." He opened a drawer as he spoke, and in half a second the cowed convict was looking down the barre of a heavy revolver.—Science Siftings

(Copyright.)

THE; DOCTOR'S ADVENTURE

By ELLA STONE,

Author of "Ring in the True," "After Long Years," "Grace Murray," "Pierre Arnaud," "Nellie Graham," &c.

"Will! W 411! There is that horrid night-bell tinkling agai9l- it is too bad." And the doctor's wife sat up in bed with a look of annoyance on her pleasant face, while the doctor sleepily turned out, and, throwing on a dressing-gown, proceeded to interview the disturber of their rest from a landing window that overlooked the surgery door. It was a bitterly cold December night, and the north-easterly gale blowing in as he opened the window made him shiver violently. A man muffled in a heavy 'cloak and wearing a sou'-wester stood on the doorstep. "Does Dr. Day live here ? " he called out in gruff accents. The doctor replied in the affirmative, and the man went on: "You've to come at once to see Mrs. Maloney, down at Pilling's Ccrve. Her lies a-dyin'." "What's the matter with her ?" asked the doctor.

• "Hey ? " said his visitor. "What's the matter with her? and which house is it ? " bawled the doctor in stentorian tones above the roar of the wind. But the man only lifted up his head, put his hand behind his ears, and reiterated interroga- tively, "Hey ? " "Confound you! Can't you hear? What's the matter with her ? " this time roared, the doctor, while the man, in equally loud tones, replied: "I'm a bit hard o' hearin', sir, but I knows nought about it, on'y I was to go and tell yer to come at once as Mrs. Maloney lay a-dyin'." The doctor closed the window with a slam and returned to his room to dress, saying to his wife, who was busying herself with a spirit-lamp and kettle to get him some hot coffee before he went out: "There's no help for it, Maggie. I've a four miles' drive before me over the cliffs to Pilling's Cove, to see some blessed old woman who 'lies a-dyin'.' That's all I can get out of the fellow. He's as deaf as a door-post." "I do call it a shame!" 'exclaimed his wife. "You were up all last night. If the woman is dying, why can't they let her die in peace. But I quite expect it is a case like that last winter, when they sent for you to the Heath at two o'clock in the morning, all through that fearful snowstorm; • and when you got there the woman was sitting over the fire and told you she'd been fair mad with toothache when she sent for you; but it was a bit easier so she'd not have anything done. She'd call at the surgery next market-day and have it out if it didna' mend.' If I'd been you, Will, I'd have insisted on pulling half-a-dozen teeth out on the spot. People have no consideration for doctors." _

"Or their wives and servants," laughed her husband, as he left the room to call his man and order him to get his horse saddled. "Do let Jones drive you in the gig," his wife called after him. "It will be better than riding, this miserable night." "No, • no," he replied. "I'd rather ride, and it's no use taking the fellow out. Get into bed again, little woman, and keep yourself warm and snug. I shall take no harm, it is all in my day's —or rather night's—work. I hope I shall be back time enough for a good snoole before I set out on my morning's round. I wish I knew where the woman lives; she must be a new-comer." But when Mrs. Day awoke, rather late the next morning, her husband had not returned. Nine o'clock came, and still he did not arrive. She rang the bell for the servant to take away the breakfast, and when the girl entered the room she was struck by her white, frightened face. "What is it, Jane ? " she asked. "Is anything the matter ? " _ _

"Oh, please, ma'am,' she said, "I'm sure you ought to be told, though Jones said we'd better say nothing about it till he'd made inquiries." "What do you mean? Tell me at once!" cried Mrs. Day, catching the infection of the girl's fears. _ . _

"Why, ma'am, when Jones went ti) the stable this morining, what should he see but master's horse, standing in the stall with saddle and bridle, just as he took it out last night." With a low, startled cry, Mrs. Day darted from the room and ran to the stable-yard, where Jones was talking to a little knot of bystanders. "What is this, Jones ? " she cried. "Where is my husband? Ride off instantly to Pilling's Cove, and inquire at Mrs. Maloney's what time he left there. Send someone to search along the cliffs." There was no lack of willing helpers, and Mrs. Wilson, the vicar's wife, who had, by this time, heard of the alarm, hastened to her friend and tried to soothe her and calm her fears. But when Jones returned and told them that no such person as Mrs. Maloney lived at Pilling's Cove, and that no one had seen or heard anything of the doctor, even the most sanguine felt that there was grave cause for alarm. _ _

Dr. Day was a general favourite in the little fishing town of Clevelys, whither he had come, about five years before the time of our story, as assistant to their old doctor, who soon afterwards died and left him in full possession of his large and somewhat scattered practice.

_ It occasioned some little heart-burning amongst the ladies of the neighbourhood when, nearly two years ago, he disappointed the hopes of match- making mothers and pretty daughters, by bringing home a wife from his own country. But Mrs. Day was a bright, pleasant little woman, who soon became as popular as her husband, and when the doctor's disappearance became generally known, the whole town shared in her anxiety and joined in the search that was made.

It transpired that the last person by whom he was seen was a coastguardsman, who met him about a mile on his way, jogging along on his quiet old mare. They had exchanged greetings, and in reply to the doctor's inquiry the man told him they were looking out for a smuggling craft which was expected to attempt a landing, but of which they could find no trace. They parted with a cheery good-night from the doctor. There were one or two dangerous places where the road went very close to the edge of the cliffs, and some deep gullies leading down to the shore, but the mystery, was, how any harm could have befallen him, and his horse remain uninjured. Had he fallen over the cliffs both must have gone together. The fact that there was no sick woman at Pilling's Cove seemed to prove that the message was sent to decoy him, and that there was foul play somewhere. Yet what could be the motive of it? Certainly not robbery, and as far as was known the doctor had not an enemy in the world. Even the rougher spirits, suspected of being leagued with the smugglers, regarded him with friendly feelings. There were few who did not owe him some debt of kindness. All through the day the suspense' continued. The police had taken up the matter, and every possible and impossible theory was discussed, while the poor wife was half frantic with grief. Just as it was growing dark she opened the front door, as she had done a dozen times before, to gaze up and down the street in the vain hope of catching a glimpse of the tall, well-known figure coming towards her, when a missile was thrown past her into the hall. Mrs. Wilson, who was with her, picked it up. It was a common india- rubber ball, but round it a narrow strip of paper was tightly wrapped and secured with a pin. They hurriedly unfastened it, and found the following words written in a rude, scrawling hand: "Seep • up 'ure spirits. 'ure 'usband is safe and will be wid u on Monday." Mysterious as were the words and the manner in which they were con- veyed, they brought hope and comfort to the wife's troubled heart. It was now Friday. Two more days and she would see him, and all would be explained, and although the efforts to 'trace him were not relaxed, she waited with a fair amount of hope. On Saturday the gale, which had been blowing for two days, increased almost to a hurricane, and the cry of a ship in distress, with the consequent launching of the life-boat, turned men's thoughts into another channel. They did not, however, succeed in reaching the vessel which, apparently, was blown out to sea. _ _ _

Who cannot imagine the feelings with which poor Mrs. Day watched and waited through the long hours of 'Monday? Alas! it was in vain. No tidings came of the missing man, and slowly and painfully hope faded from his wife's heart. Days passed on, and Christmas came, with its joyous meetings and tender associations. But the bells from the old church tower at Clevelys did not chime their accustomed peal. They had been stopped by the vicar's orders, for over the .doctor's house, hard by, the angels of life and death were hovering. A tiny life had dawned prematurely into a sorrowful world, to pass away again into the dim 111Vettrious darknen from whence A came; and the

mother did not weep over the loss of her first-born 1 child—she so longed to pass with it into that land where there are no partings. Her mother, Mrs. Bromfield, who had come to I her in her trouble, was with her, and the young I man who had been sent for from one of the London hospitals to carry on the doctor's work, as locum tenens, with a more experienced practitioner from a neighbouring town, did all they could for her, but they rooked very grave. "If the child had lived it might have roused her; she would have wished to live for its sake," said the young doctor to Mr. Wilson, "but now she is just sinking; and unless something very unforeseen happens, there is no chance of her rallying." But the unforeseen did happen! All through Christmas week Mrs. Day lay in a half-unconscious state, taking no notice of anything, but, as it seemed, quietly waiting for the end, which, to the anxious watchers, did not appear to be far off. New Year's Eve came, and Mrs. Wilson was sharing Mrs. Bromfield's watch in the sick-room. The night was frosty, and the sound of passing footsteps rang out clear and distinct on the hard groimd. There were many people passing to and fro, and they did not notice a firm, decided foot- step coming rapidly down the street and pausing at their door; but they were startled by seeing the sufferer suddenly sit up in bed, exclaiming in a voice clear and strong almost as when in health: "Hark! That is his footstep! He has come home —my husband—my husband. Let me go to him!" They rushed to her side, believing her raving, but the street door closed, the footsteps quickly ascended the stairs, and Maggie Day, shaking off their restraining arms, sprang out of bed, and reached the door of the room in time to fall faint- ing and unconscious into her husband's arms. lie carried her in, and laid her down tenderly, and for some time they were all too much engrossed with her to ask any questions. The old year, which had brought, such grievous trouble to that happy home, passed away, and the New Year, in which joy and sorrow hung trembling in the balance, dawned; and still the anxious watchers could see no sign of consciousness in the white, silent form lying on the bed. She still lived, and that was all they could say; but her husband, who had gathered from the young doctor's answers to his few hasty questions, full particulars as to her state, never gave up hope nor relaxed for one moment his efforts to restore her. At last he was rewarded by hearing a faint, quivering sigh. The eyelids un- closed, and a low voice murmured: "Will, are you here? Am I dreaming ? " "Yes, I am here, my darling," he answered, bending over her and clasping her hand fondly, but carefully restraining all sign of emotion for fear of agitating her.. "0 Will, I had such a horrid 'dream," she said faintly. "I thought you had gone quite away; kiss me, dear, and hold my hand fast that I may be quite sure it was a dream, and then I shall go to sleep again." And as he sat by her side holding her hand firmly clasped in his, her eyes closed again and the calm, regular breathing shewed that she had fallen into a peaceful sleep. "She will do now," the doctor said, turning to the others with a sigh of relief. "You may go to rest, and leave her to me."

They wanted him to go and have some refresh- ment, but he refused, saying-: ."No, she must not awake to find me absent. Thank God I have arrived in time to save her."

Some weeks of suspense still lay before them, i weeks of anxious nursing and ceaseless devotion on her husband's part, before Mrs. Day was out of !danger and able to hear from his lips the story of the strange adventure which had nearly cost him so dear, and which we will now relate in a more con- secutive fashion than he was able to do.

On that memorable night, he had not long parted with the friendly coastguardsman, and had just reached one of the gullies leading down to' the shore known by the ill-omened name of Deadman's Gully, supposed by the country folk to be haunted, when his arms were suddenly seized from behind, he was dragged from his horse, and a cloak thrown over his head which served the double purpose of blind- folding him and smothering his cries for help. All was done so quickly that he had no chance of resistance; he did struggle to free himself as his captors proceeded to bind his hands and feet, but he found himself held with an iron grip, though they seemed anxious not to hurt him, and one man whispered reassuringly: "Doan.'t 'ee fear, doctor, we means no harm by 'ee."

He was then carried down the gully, and laid, ;still blinded and bound, at the bottom of a small {boat, which was evidently in waiting, and rapidly rowed out to sea. In a short time the boat stopped, ,and he felt himself hauled up on board a ship of some kind. In a few minutes more his bonds were Iremoved, and he saw, by the light of the moon, ,which had just emerged from a dark bank of clouds, ,that he was on the deck of a small schooner, sur- rounded by a number of rough-looking men, whom Ihe at once recognised as smugglers. One, who was apparently the captain, a bold, rakish-looking fellow, making some pretensions to gentility, ad- dressed him politely, hat in hand, and apologised for the violence that had been used towards him. "You see, doctor," he said, "necessity has no law—we required your services immediately, and were unable to summon you in the usual way. I had hoped to be able to put your patient ashore, but we had news that our good friends of the Custom House were making themselves unpleasantly busy, and that it was not advisable to do so • so there was nothing for it but to fetch you. Will you walk this way," and he led the way to a small cabin, dimly lighted by an oil lamp, in one corner of which, on a heap of rugs, lay a boy of ten or eleven years of age, moaning, and evidently in great pain. •

"Cheer up, Dick," said the smuggler, going up to him, "here's the doctor come, and he'll soon put you as right as ninepence." The lad turned a pair of large, dark, expressive eyes on the doctor, so full of pathetic, wistful appeal, that his heart was touched at once. "He had a fall this morning from the riggint said the captain, "and has hurt his leg somehow —hip put out I fear. He's a brave, good little chap, though he is my own son. You'll do what you can for him doctor ? " The doctor carefully and tenderly examined his patient, and, finding it was as his father supposed, proceeded to put in the joint and bandage the limb, the boy a singularly refined, delicate.. looking child— bearing the severe pain with much' fortitude.

"There," he said, as he re-arranged his rugs and laid him down comfortably upon them 'your father was quite right. You are a brave boy, now you will have to be a patient one, for I am afraid you will have to lie here for some time. But I'll tell you what I'll do.. When your father puts me ashore just now, I'll send you a lot of picture- books to help to pass the time." The boy looked up with a grateful smile, but his father said:

"Very sorry, doctor, to lutve to inconvenience you, but we shall have to keep you here a little while longer. The revenue cutter is cruising about, and we must make ourselves scarce. I hope, by Monday, we may be able to accommodate you." In vain Dr. Day remonstrated and stormed, knowing what anxiety and inconvenience his absence would cause. The only comfort he got was from an old sailor, whom he recognised as having lived at Pilling's Cove, and as the man who had spoken to him when he was first captured:

"Doan't 'ee fret, doci-or," he said; "I've not forgot all as you did for my old woman when her wer' bad last winter, and, says I, his missis shall know as he's safe, so I give my lad a slip of paper to take her, sayin' as how you're all right, and 'nil be home a-Monday." Somewhat consoled by this. and perfectly power- less to help himself, the d6ctor had to make the best of things, and devoted himself to amusing his Patient. Then came the terrible storm, which swept over the ;Ride vessel, carrying away mast and Ails, and leafing only a shattered hulk to drift helplessly on at the mercy of wind and sea. For four days they were'tossed about, and poor Dick would have fared badly but for be ceaseless care and attention of his kind friend. At last, when hope seemed almost th.v were by an American line.

No Breakfast Table complete without
