WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The Removal of Smallpox Patients to Sankey.

The monthly meeting of this Council was held at Bewsey Chambers on Wednesday afternoon under the presidency of Mr. T. Halton. There were also present: —Mes srs. Brooks, Ball, Mather, Houghton, Forshaw, Dr. Sephton (medical officer of health), Messrs. J. C. Sutton (clerk), I'. Kerfoot (inspector), B. S. Newcombe (surveyor), and F. Burnage (assistant surveyor).

THE moToR. CAR ACT

The CLERK -reported the receipt of a letter from the Clerk to the County Council informing him that the Main Roads and Bridges Committee had passed the following resolution :—That this committee, while be- ing of opinion that the safety of the public will in most cases be sufficiently protected by Section 1 of the Motor Car Act, 1903. are prepared to consider the application of any local authority for a ten mile an hour limit, and will investigate each case upon its merits, and report to the Local Government Board their decision."

. 'The CLERK said he took it that the Council would approve of the general resolution without asking for any special limitation in their distriet. The SURVEYOR pointed out that section 1 of the act made it an offence for any person to drive reck- lessly, negligently, or in a manner dangerous to the public.

HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT. Dr. SEPHTON, who was congratulated by the Council upon his restoration to health, ,reported that during the month ending December 2nd there had been 2G births and 35 deaths. The death 'rate was equal to 37.8 per 1,000 against 34, the average for the previous three months, and 23 for the corresponding period last year, Twenty seven deaths occurred in the County Asylum at Winwick, and exclusive of these the death rate proper for the district was 86. Durinz the past month 18 births and 38 deaths had been notified. The death rate was equal to 41'2 against 35 the average for the past three months and 357 for the corresponding month last year. Exclusive of the deaths at the asylum and Sankey Sanatorium, the rate for the district was 8•6.

LONGFORD DEPOT. The INSPECTOR reported that in accordance with instructions he had taken observations at Longford Depot, during the past month, but no improvement had taken place as far as the nuisance was concerned.- The previous day, when he visited the works, he found bacteria beds being laid down for experimental pur- poses, a condensing tower was also being built, and a large reservoir was to be constructed to store water for condensing purposes. It was also proposed to put up another steam boiler for motive power. Mr, HOUGHTON: Do you think the last proposal is a serious one?

The IN—s-r-F;crrort replied in the affirmative and said the Corporation were now doing what he and Dr. Sephton had suggested. Dr. SEPHTON said he had for a long time loeen asking the authorities to put down a condensing tank. Previously they had refused to do so, on the ground of expense and deficiency of water. Now, however, they were going to carry out. the work and the effect would be to do away with the smell from the chimney. In the past he had had to complain of the emptying of the contonts of the boilers on the floor and this, he believed, would also be remedied.

The matter then dropped, it being understood that the Inspector would continue to take observations.

THE REMOVAL OF SMALL-PDX PATIENTS TO SANKEY. The CHAIRMAN called attention to complaints which had been made of the conveyance of patients to Sankey Sanatorium along Back-lane. This was considered detrimental to the district. Dr. Gornall, however, seemed to take no notice of the matter.although he(Mr. Halton) spoke of it three or four months ago, and letters had appeared in the Press. The patients could be taken along the high road, where there was not a single house. The high road was also the more direct. On the proposition of the CHAIRMAN, seconded by Mr. MATHER, it was decided that the Clerk should write to the Town Clerk recommending that patients should be taken along the high road. This was all the business of public interest.

HOW MALARIA IS SPREAD.

Interesting Lecture by Mr. E. E. Lowe.

"Mosquitoes and Malaria " was the subject of an interesting lecture delivered on Monday evening by Mr. E. E. Lowe. F.L.S. in the Museum Lecture Room. The chair was occupied Alderman Bennett, who in introducing Mr. Lowe said he hardly needed an intro- duction to many Warrington people, who had had the privilege of knowing him for some time. When Mr. Lowe left Warrington to fill an important appointment at Plymouth, they all regretted the necessary severance, although they felt he deserved the honour which had been conferred upon him an honour which, to a certain extent cast a reflected glory upon them. He was always particularly pleased to hear of a fellow townsman's success in the world. (Hear, hear, and applause.) The subject Mr. Lowe had selected was one of great interest. He had had thei pleasure of reading something on the same subject from Mr. Lowe's pen in a magazine in which he (the chairman) was interested, and the way in which they were endeavouring to trace the cause of some of the diseases which afflicted a portion of the earth's surface, and eradicate them, spoke volumes for the pro- gressive notions of this particular age. He was an idealist, as most of them know, and he hoped that some clay every specific disease would be hunted to its lair, and that disease would become a thing of the past. He did not suppose that he or any of the audience would live to see that day—(laughter)—but any investigation that was made into the cause of disease that would help them to remove the preventable causes of misery should receive a most hearty welcome. (Hear, hear.) Mr. LOWE, who hail a cordial reception, expressed the hope that Alderman Bennett's concluding remarks were prophetic. When the Museum Committee paid him the compliment of asking him to deliver a lecture he had great difficulty in deciding what he should talk about to acritical Warrington audience. It seemed to him that the question of mosquitoes and malaria was so much in the air at the present that it might be interesting, and perhaps useful, if he discussed the subject with a certain amount of detail, because since he had been at Plymouth he had paid some attention to it. In order to convey a clear idea to their minds it would be necessary to divide the question into four parts—(l), to considerthe life history and structure of mosqultoes; (2), to go into the characteristics of malaria; (3), to trace the history of the connection between the first two divisions of the question; and (4), to say a few words about the im- portance of the subject. In dealing with his subject under the first head, Mr. Lowe showed a number or diagrams, illustrating the structure of mosquitoes or gnats, and pointed out the difference between the harmless and dangerous ones In an interesting way, Mr. Lowe traced the history of malaria and referred to the steps which led up to the medical discoveries that malaria was traceable to the mosquito. It was discovered, he said, that one of the most characteristic features of malaria was that a black substance called melanin was always present in the blood of patients suffering from malaria. By patient research it was found that the melanin was due to a parasite which entered the system and broke up in the plasma. The parasites took different forms, but they all belonged to the very lowest forms of organic life. One form was called a sporocyte, from the fact that it produced spores, and another was called gametocyte Reference was rmade to the discoveries of Major IF Ross, of Liverpool, and others, that these parasites were introduced into the blood by mosquitoes who obtained them in their foo d from puddles and swampy districts. When a per- son was bitten by a mosquito, there was a danger of these parasites getting into the blood and setting up malaria. As showing the importance of the subject Mr. Lowe pointed out that in 1897, the British army in Indiaconsisted of 178,000 men, and in one year out of that number 75,000 were admitted to hospital suffering from malarial fever. With regard to the civil popula- tion in India the deaths from malaria alone were 5,096,725 ! Some of the deaths included natives who. did not undergo adequate treatment. India .vas not one of the most malarial i countries, the West Coast of Africa was very much worse. He thought they would agree with him that the theory he had propounded that evening, was a most important one. Great objections bad been raised to it, but when it came to be better understood he had no doubt the result of the investiga- tion would be beneficial to the world at large as it had already proved interesting to scientific students. Very often zoology and kindred sciences were scoffed at, but he wanted to show that they might have some bearing on their daily life. He hoped he had succeeded to some extent in ',carrying out that object. (Hear, hear, and applause.) The Cif A.IRM AN observed that he did not think the audience had been disappointed. (Hear, hear.)Mr.Lowe had the gift of lucid explanation, and his lecture had been to Mai a great treat. He had obviously made a close study of the subject. He could not help thinking that the mosquito was a deadlier enemy to the British Empire than the Boers. They might do worse than equip an army of naturalists to do battle against the mosquito. (Laughter.) He would like to ask what was the proper method to adopt? Was it proposed to do away with the mosquito? (Laughter.) If that was so, he was afraid it was a big order. (Hear, hear.) Mr. T. MAY, in proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. Lowe, said he was specially interested in the lecture, because a friend of his had recently returned from West Africa, after having suffered from .malarial fever. Fortunately, he had been almost entirely restored to health, although at onetime he was in such a weak condition that he had to be carried on board tie vessel. Mr. May proceeded to refer to the eminent school of naturalists in Warrington. There was a Mr. Greening of a former generation, who collected natural history specimens and his son, Mr. Linmeus Greening, who had been long connected with the Field Club, and had given them a very valuable collection of snakes and spiders, which could be mien by request at any time. Mr. Lowe would acknowledge that it was greatly through the kindness of Mr. Greening that he was en- couraged to study the interesting subject he had brought under their notice that evening. Mr. May con- cludeil by asking whether there were any mosquitoes in this part of the country at the present time as he was recently bitten by a knat at Lower Walton which caused a large lump to rise on the ankle. Mr. BINDLOSS in seconding the motion said he could appreciate the importance of the lecture, because he had haJ an experience of mosquitoes in all parts of the world. There was nothing that was a more deadly enemy to mankind than the dreadful little creature to which Mr. Lowe had referred. They could not do sufficient honour to that famous school in Liverpool which was doing so much to stamp out, not only the mosquito, but malaria in all parts of the world. (hear,hear.) - Mr. G. Carruthers thought Mr. Lowe ought to devote a great deal more of his time to teaching, because he possessed all the admirable qualities the best of teach- ers should possess. He had never listened to a school- master who could show in such a clear and straight- forward way the main points of a subject, that he had taken up. Nowadays they were impressed with the number of new diseases. They had one prominent disease they could not i find n certain dictionaries. He referred to appendicitis which was a new word for an old disease called stomach ache—(laughter)—but in malaria they had a very old disease which seemed to have come back again. Two of the most prominent rulers that England had had, died of malaria—Oliver Cromwell and Henry IV. In those days it was called ague. If Robert Bruce did not die from it he spent a miserable life through it, but in those days there were more marshes than there were now. Was it a fact that if they could live in a three. storey building on the West Coast of Africathey would never be bothered with mosquitoes, and, therefore,never , suffer from malaria?

CUL S all FAIN, HEALS

A RECKLESS SPEECH

SINCE REPENTED OF.

MR. CHAMBERLAIN'S SPEECH AT THE CONSTITUTIONAL.

===== 0002601_19040102_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
In June Last.

Mu. CHAMBERLAIN has not republished his speech delivered at the Constitutional Club on June 26th, 1903. It is strangely omitted from the bound volume of his speeches, from May 15th to November 4th, on the Fiscal question. We propose to do something towards supplying the omission by recalling the leading passages. The speech naturally divides itself into three parts: (1) What we ought to do for the Colonies. (2)What the Colonies do for us. (3) What we shall have to pay for Preference, and how we shall pay it.

===== 0002601_19040102_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
A Prelimirary Declaration

MR. CHAMBERLAIN prefaced his speech by a somewhat violent declaration against Home Rule. He seemed to have forgotten that the Government of which he was a member was then busily occupied in conceding to "Irish rebels" a far more sweeping reform than Home Rule that of handing over the laud to the Irish peasants. Loyalty at 25. per !lead.

ON June 26th Mr. Chamberlain said: During the last eight years we hare been engaged in seeking to bind together and build no the Empire.

Apparently the attempt has not been very successful up to the present, as Mr. Chamber- lain had to admit, after all these years, that a system of tariffs is the only system by which this Empire can be kept together.

That does not speak very well for the success c f Mr. Chamberlain's "aCm.nistration of the Col on ies. " Colonial loyalty, according to the late Colonial Secretary, must be at a very low ebb. His scheme of Preference would give the Colonists about 2s. a head per year, less than i. per Colonist per week. Unless we tax the bread of the poor to give this preference. Mr. Chamberlain says the Empire will fall into disjointed atoms. Colonial loyalty must be a very cheap commodity in Mr. Chamber- lain's estimation. What We Ought to do.

MR. CHA3IDERLAIN tells us we have asked the Colonies to come into a closer union, and th 9, have refused. He says we have asked them to form a union for the defence of the Empire, and they have said they prefer to remain independent. So, says he, if we are to bind up the Empire "we must consult the Colonies." "We must walk in the way that is most consonant to their interests and their desires." The picture which Mr. Chamberlain presents of the relations between the Colonies and the Mother Country is not a pleasant one; it is not a true one, and it is grossly unfair to the Colonies.

What the Colonies do for Us. EVERY Free Trader appreciates the loyalty of the Colonies, and the advantages which this country derives from Colonial trade. We only deprecate the attempts being made to place the self-governing Colonies in a position which, politically and financially, they have never held and never wish to hold. When such attempts are made, it becomes necessary to state in plain terms the exact truth of the .matter. ' For good or evil the self-governing Colonies have adopted Protection. They , have repeatedly told us that, whatever changes they may make in response to Preferential Tariffs, they will not lower their duties to a level which would allow British goods to , compete with their own. In the course of this ! speech Mr. Chamberlain stbt d that ten thousand of our own kinsmen take from ' us at the present time £lO per head. The facts are somewhat different. The popula- tion of our .self-governing Colonies is 6,104,000, and we sent them last year £61,563,000 worth of our goods, while foreigners and others sent them £62,191,000. Mr. Chamberlain claims that by Preferential tariffs we could capture the foreigners' trade. As a matter of fact, however, the greater part of the goods sent by foreigners to the Colonies are such as this country does not, and could not, produce.

Paying for Preference. WHEN Mr. Chamberlain first launched his scheme, he said:

This policy, if carried out, will cost the working man so much more a week than at prcsent. . . If you give a preference to the Colonies you must put a tax upon food.

But he soon found out that a tax upon food was not exactly popular! The working man did not seem to be specially fascinated at the prospect of paying so much a week more in order to put money into the pockets of a few Colonial growers and English landlords. So Mr. Chamberlain performed a right-about-face, and angrily declared he never proposed any- thing of the, sort. He said : say it is monstrous to suppose that a party (Tory) with such a record would now come forward to impose greater burdens upon the poor. He then proceeded to explain how he was going to tax goods without increasing the cost of living. This was his scheme :

If the increa;ed cost of bread and meat is met by a proportionate decrease in some other articles, although the price of a particular article may be raised, the cost of living will not be increased in the slightest degree. . . . If, for instance, they are called upon to pay 3d. a week additional on the cost of their bread, they may he fully, entirely relieved by a reduction in the cost of their tea, their sugar, or even of their tobacco. It is perfectly clear that this idea of balanc- ing the increased cost of living of the working classes was a second thought. Mr. Chamberlain first of all proposed to provide Old-Ago Pensions as a return for the increased price of food. It was only when he found dear food was unpopular that he proposed to balance the cost of living by reducing the duties on tea, sugar, and tobacco. It is also clear that he had never thought the matter out, because a reduction in the tea, sugar, and tobacco duties would make a big hole in the national Purse. Mr. Chamberlain had forgotten that. Later on he had to propose a tax upon our goods to equalise the money he wanted to take out of the Treasury to pay for the tax on our food. But, of course, you ,antiot make up for dear bread, pudding, meat, and dairy produce by giving cheaper tQa, sugar, and tobacco. Besides, what advant..ge will it be to buy cheaper tea, sugar, or . tobacco when Mr. Chamberlain's tax on good vill increase the price of our clothes, household goods, and rent ? It is not surprising this ill-considered speech was suppressed by its author.

TurEspAssisi; rs SEArwrr OF GAME.

At Runcorn on Monday, -Arthur Fletcher. of Frod- sham,was charged with trespassing on land in the occupa- tion of Joseph Astbury, at Halton. in search of game. Joseph Houghton, gamekeeper to Sir Richard Brooke, spoke to the defendant being on the land with a grey hound dog. The defendant said he did not know he was trespassing, as there was a cart road across the land. Witness said it was a driving lane for the farmers only.—The defendant, who had a bad record, was fined 10s. and costs or fourteen days' hard labour.

PLEASANT PARAGRAPIES.

ANYBODY who knows enough can learn some- thing from anybody else. MRS. HOPEWELL : "But, George, what is a brio- let diamond?" Mr. Hopewell: "One like a pear." Mrs. Hopewell "Well, then, George, why not buy me a pair and have done with it." AN AFTERTHOUGLIT.—Visitor : "Is there any- thing I can do for you?" Prisoner: "Y ou might send me a file of newspapers—or any old file."

THE HARDEST PART.—Unclo : "So ye've been studyin' art, hey? Well, hey ye learnt the busi. ness ?'' Nephew: "Not quite, uncle. I haven't learned how to sell my pictures yet." .

MRS. SKIMPEN : think Mr: Smith must have liked the beefsteak pie. He had two helpings of it." The Tactless Boarder: "Possibly he did it for a wager." IN DISPEITE.—" I understand they fell out the day after they were married." "Yes. The nem.- papers gave.a column notice of their wedding, and they disputed as to whether it was because of the prominance of his family or of hers." MR. WHINGLEBY : "Oh, you needn't feel so uppish. You're not the only girl in Peckham." Miss Flashleigh : "No, I'm not the only girl in Peckham who has publicly announced that she wouldn't have you if you were the last man on earth." _ _

The Russian did try, but after innumerable misses he turned round to one of the bystanders and said: " Ach, monsieur, it would be a very nice game if the ball were ten times larger. Now let us go home.'

SAVED.—As he was about to sink for the third time, he of course recalled everything in his past. life. His countenance radiated with joy. "Ah !" he exclaimed; "since I now remember what it was my wife told me to get to-day, I have no further occasion to drown." And he swam ashore.

"WHAT if I were one of those husbands, my dear, who get up cross in the morning and bang things about and kick everything over just because the coffee is cold ?" "John," responded his wife, "1 would make it hob for him." As her words admitted of more than one interpretation, John said nothing about the coffee. "DID you know that Miss Bjones was going to marry young Smith ?" "I knew it; but I cannot understand how a girl as intelligent as she is cab concoct to marry a man stupid enough to want on marry her." WISHED IT WAS.—" It lb unutterably sad," she said, "unutterably sad," and went on talking about it. "I wish it was," remarked her husband, when he had a chance to speak. "Was what!" she inquired in astonishment. "Unutterably sad," and there was a lull. AT CROSS PURPOSES.--" Billies is a pleasant sort of chap," remarked one young man. "I don't like him," said the other. "He's too egotistic." "It's merely a matter of impression. You judged him from his manner that he's egotistic and you feel resentful. He judges from your manner that you think he's egotistic, and he resents your self- opinionated fashion of jumping at conclusions. It's a mis;Ake all round."

SINGLETON: "Have you ever experienced the supreme joy that comes from finding in your pocket half-a-crown that you didn't know you had ? " Benedict: "No; but my wife has upon several occasions."

"I TELL you," observed Mr. Oldboy, who was In a moralising frame of mind, "it takes a great deal of courage to say 'No." "You're always brave enough to say it when I ask you for money," said Mrs. Oldboy. "Tae average life of an engine only thirty years?" said an astonished passenger. "Why, such a tough-looking thing ought to live longer than that." "Well," said the driver, "perhaps it would if it didn't smoke so much."

THE TRUE CONDITION.—Briggs: "Do you be- lieve that the world is divided into two classes those who borrow and those who lend ?" Griggs: "No, sir. My experience is than two other classes are much more prevalent—those who want to borrow and those who won't lend." MRS. WORRYTON : "I wish I could fathom something whereby I could keep my husband at home at nights." Mrs. Sootherson : "C. 3 t him a bicycle." Mrs. Worryton : "That would take him out more than ever." Mrs. Sootherson: "Oh, no, it wouldn't. My husband got one the other day, and the doctor says he won't be out for a month.

i-irr old Highlander, on his first visit to Glasgow, was filling his pipe, when he was approached by a match-boy with the usual "Lights, sir?" The aid. man innocently took the box from the boy, ex- tracted a match, lit his pipe, and handing the box back to the astonished lad, passed on with the re- mark: "Weel, weal, what a wonderfu' place Glas- gow is tae be sure!" biairsort had refused to get his wife a new hat, and shortly afterwards his little girl came in and said: "Mamma, will you buy me a monkey to 'play with when you go to town?" "No, my darling. Wait till you are older, and then marry one, as I did," replied the grief-stricken wife, her tears bursting forth afresh. AFTER he had kissed her and pressed her rosy cheek against his, and patted her soft round chin, she drew back, and asked : "George, do you shave yourself?" "Yes," he replied. "I thought so," she said. "Your face is the roughest I ever----" Then she stopped, buf it was too late, and he went away with a cold heavy lnmp in his hreast. WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Mr. Bent Suggests the Boarding.out

of Children.

Arneeting of the above named Board was held on Wednes day morning. Mr. A. Brooks presided and there were also present:—Mrs. Clay,Dr.Patterson,Messrs.S.Davies, J. Payton, A. Joßeg, A. J. Jolley, G. Pilling, T. H. Ashcroft, William Hickman, J. Billinge, R. Forshaw, W. Houghton, J. Turton, G. Mather, T. Ritchie, J. ;Robinson, E. L. Bent, and J. C. Sutton (clerk). J. F. Awbary (workhouse master), A. Wright (superintendent Padgate Schools.)

THE CHRISXMAS TREITS.

The Superintendent of Padgate Schools and the Work- house Master reported that the treats as directed by the Guardians were given on Christmas Day. The Workhouse Master reported that he was to express the gratitude and appreciation of every soul in the place. Everything passed off admirably. There was not the slightest hitch nor any miscondset and he was sure this would be gratifying information to the Board as it • was his proud privilege to report. The officers also desired him to thank the Board for their seasonable generosity. • CWANGES IN THE NURSING STAFF. The House Committee recommended the appointment of Mr. J. W. Follows, as labour master, and Miss Bray as night charge nurse. The resignations of probationer nurses Stringer and Dutton were accepted and Miss Louisa Sutch and Miss Mary Tynam, were appointed probationer nurses. It was suggested that Miss Mary Aird, of Blackburn and Miss Eliza Bailey, of • Croydon, two candidates fbr the office of day charge • nurse appear before the Mad. The Medical Officer of 1 the Workhouse was instructed, in view of the epidemic of smallpox, to examine all vagrants and to offer free t vaccination. The terms for such services were re- •' served. It was recommended that the tender of Mr. W. Winstanley for the supply of a cylinder ,be accepted. 1 Mr. G. PILLING moved the confirmation of the ; minutes.

Mr. S. DAVIES seconded and the minutes were passed.

APPOINTMENT OF A DAY CHARGE NURSE. In accordance with the House Committee's recom- mendation, two candidates appeared before the Board for the position of day charge nurse. The two candi- dates were Miss Mary Aird, of Blackburn, and Miss' Eliza Bailey, of Croydon. - -

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

Authors of letters intended for publication must inall cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter inteuded for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible,not later than Thursday.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Mr. R. W. MURRAY'S letter on the fiscal problem, which appeared in our issue of December 19th, con- tained a sentence to the effect that fifty years ago he "found England happy and prosperous with a poptila- tion well-nigh double what it is to-day, and politically, content." The country should have been "Ireland.".

Warrngton Bridge: The Proposed Widening.

Sir,—As reported in your last week's issue, the General Purposes Committee has decided to recommend to the Town Council a scheme to relieve the traffic on Warrington Bridge and prevent accidents to the public, by throwing out a cantilever footpath on each side, and making the present bridge into a roadway for convey- ances only, at an expense, say, of from £3,000 to £4,000. In my opinion, this is a useless expenditure of public money, without promising any permanent improvement, and I ask you to allow me to place my views before the ratepayers before the Council come to any decision, as I believe that this is a matter that should not be finally decided until the, ratepayers are consulted and their views made kncrwn.

lam prepared to admit that the present bridge is not sufficient to accommodate the traffic between Cheshire and Lancashire that passes through our town. It is not a suitable connection between both parts of the borough, but the proposed widening by outside footpaths would be only a useless attempt to make the present bridge fulfil our requirements. What we need is three bridges, and three different and separate con- nections between the two banks of the river in town. The lack of these three connections has been, in my opinion, one of the great drawbacks to our town's ad- vance and prosperity this last twenty years. What we badly require is a bridge across the river at Howley Quay into Latchford, and another bridge at Walton, connected with the proposed road across Arpley Meadows, making that one of tke finest approaches it ispossible to get into Warrington from Cheshire; but I intend to deal only with the Howley Quay Bridge in my present letter. When the present bridge was opened, over 70 years ago, the population of Warrington was less than 20,000, and the population of Latchford was counted in hun- dreds ; in fact, 'Latchford at that time was only a small straggling village of cottages huddled together.in the neighbourhood of'Bridge-foot. Now our population is over 60,000, and the population of Latchford alone is about 8,000 and it is a large and growing town in itself, with a frontage along the river of several miles. It can be easily understood that the one bridge that served the town 70 years ago, and was sufficient for the town's requirements then, is not sufficient for our present needs, no matter how wide it may be. made, or even if a new bridge is built on the site of the old one. In order to increase the ratable cattle of the town, which is the proper way to reduce, the rates, it is necessary to 'peke permanent improvements in our various districts, and not to spend all our !honey on one or two favoured centres ; and Howley, Fairfield, and St John's wards, with the great thoroughfares of Church „street and Manchester road, need consideration. Several years ago the Town Council purchased, with the ratepayers's money, the Old Warps estate, at a cost of £lO,OOO with the avowed intention of making it into a public park and recreation ground, but of what earthly use is it as it is, without an approach and entrance other than the one in Knutsford-road? The part of our town most in need of a park and recreation ground is that comprised in the densely populated dis- tricts of St. John's, Fairfield, and Howley wards. These areas could be brought into close touch with Latchford if a bricUe were built at Howley Quay, but it is a day's journey to reach Victoria Park and return home from the neighbourhood of the Cemetery at present. I believe the purchase of the Old Warps estate was a first-class bargain for the Corporation, and the purpose it was bought for was the right purpose; but under •existing conditions it is quite useless to us and a dead 'loss, for, I take it, we are paying interest on the pur- , chase money at the rate of 2:1 or. per cent., while we' are not receiving a return of 2 per cent., when the house and grounds are fully let, which is very seldom. Then, why not make a bridge across the river at, Howley Quay into Victoria Park, and a road through the Park to Knutsford-road, giving the above districts an easy access to our park. and another read out of Lancashire into Cheshire, relieving our now existing bridge of at least one-eighth of the present traffic? Besides, this would help to make Church-street a first- class business thoroughfare, instead of a street of common cottage property. But some people will say: The present bridge is dangerous and must be altered in :any case. I admit the bridge is inconvenient, but it is all nonsense to say it is dangerous. The length of the ' 'bridge is so shoat that, with the improved wide Approaches neither drivers nor pedestrians could be injured except through their own culpable carelessness It is a well-known fact that the old bridge will be an obstruction to navigation as soon as the new river diversion is completedand the surface of the water has been raised by the dam; so that to spend the small ,estimated sum of £3,000 on footpaths is simply to throw that sum away on temporary and unnecessary improvements. The need for another bridge at Walton, and a road' through Arpley Meadows. I intend to discuss at some future date; but, in the meantime. I hope some of your numerous readers will give us their views on the sub- jects I have raised. as I feel theta matterof such public importance as the badly required bridge cannot be too widely discussed and explained to the ratepayers who will be palled upon topay for them.—yours. &c., JOHN MAGINN.

But the amount of her annual drainage of gold from the United States far exceeds that from India. Therefore, the United States is by far the most valuable of all the dependencies of Great Britain. In the relation of England to India, there is something pitiable, because India is helpless. In the relation of the United States to England there is nothing that is not contemptible, because it is the willing servitude of a nation that could help herself if she would. England is wide awake to these conditions, and keenly appreciates the priceless value to her. The United States blinks at them, half dazed, half asleep, insensible of their tremendous damage to her. England, clearly seeing that, in this age more than ever before, ocean empire is world empire, -strains every nerve to perpetuate her sea power and exhausts her resources to double rivet the •fetters which it fastens upon mankind. 'After this follows abuse of England quite in the Tory style of those countries on whom we are invited to retaliate, for our treatment of foreign shipping is thus described with ludicrous untruth She will crush it by intrigue, by cajolery, by treaties, if she can. She will crush it by prepon- derating force if she must. Ever since two first-class American ships were put in the transatlantic' trade wider American management, every device of foul play that selfish Ingenuity can invent and every resort that unscru- pulous rivalry can suggest have been exhausted by the English Press and the English Administration to defame and discredit them. English officials abroad,- from Ministers and Co- nsuls down, industriously reproduce in the news- papers of Japan, China, Chili' Argentine, and Brazil the misstatements of the English Press about American vessels. The British Post-office delays the American mails for days in the slower ships of the Cunard line irather than send so much as one letter by the American line. Our Post-office responds by liberal allotments of its European mails to all the British lines. . The result of all this is that while this country has neverknowu such financial distress England has never known such industrial activity and financial prosperity as now.' Does it not occur to men who look the least bit below the surface that the warfare for ocean em- pire. and the strife for commanding sea powers -which England forces upon the rest of mankind have reached a stage so acute that her prosperity -unalterably means the misery of everybody else,and that everybody's loss is inevitably her gain? Then comes the question as to whether the United States is to take this treatment lying down. What is the response of the United States to this tremendous exertion of English energy and resource to the aggrandisement of her sea power? Now. when I was over in the United States, I made the acquaintance of Mr. Hanna. a man with all Mr. Chamberlain's characteristic points, and one who could command, for the purposes of agitation, even greater financial backing than lies at the disposal of Mr Chamberlain in the secret funds of the Tariff Reform League.- I should not be surprised if Mr. Hanna did not succeed in imitating Mr. Chamberlain and successfully weac'h a doctrine of compensation to the shipping in- terests fon.the "tariff reforms." which were the cause of their downfall. Y ours faithfully, CARL-VON BELLAIRS. Dec. 23rd, 1903.

THE NEW YEAR.

HOW IT WAS USHERED IN AT WARRINGTON.

The dawn of the year 1904 was heralded at War- rington by the joyful pealing of church bells and the shrill, harsh notes of what are familiarly known as works' "buzzers." The solemnity of the' hour when the old year ebbs out and we stand upon the thres- hold of another year, with all its possibilities, was impressed upon those who assembled in the various places of worship where watchnight services were held. Here addresses--in which retrospect and anticipation were the domin- ant notes—were delivered and the last few minutes of 1903 were spent in silent prayer. After the customary peal of welconie, the Parish Church bells rang out the National Anthem. The congrega- tions dispersing from; the churches and chapels ex- changed hearty greetings and many were the wishes for mutual happiness and prosperity. Yesterday many townspeople kept holiday, the principal shops being closed. In the evening there were numerous social functions, including tea meetings in connec- tion with .several Nonconformist churches, the Technical Institute students' soiree, and the :much- appreciated annual treat to the old] folks at Stockton Heath.

WATCH NIGHT SERVICES.

===== 0002601_19040102_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe Congregational Church. "A Black Letter Year."

A fairly-large congregation assembled in Wycliffe Congregational Church an the last hour of 1903 on the Occasion of the annual watch-night service. The Rev. John Yonge (pastor) conducted the service. Suitable hymns were sung, the organ being presided at by Master Pearce.

I The Rev. JOHN YONGE, in a seasonable address, said f their thoughts in the closing minutes of a year—especi- ally when gathered in the House of God—inevitably were ledalong one almost unvarying line. Retrospect was compulsory. Perforce, they had to look back, at least over the closing year; but also in some measure over life's whole expanse. Speaking of 1903, which now was marking its latest pulses, the fact that they were spared to witness its close gave occasion for thankfulness tO- God. Taken altogether, however, he fancied that 1903 would be remembered mostly, gener- ally, as a very disappointing year. Take the outermost fact of all—it had been a year of bad weather. The badness of its weather had achieved a "record" degree, exceeding anything known for a long period. The rains had been persistent, until the country had been flooded, even the great towns had had streets and districts under water. London itself had been deluged' in many parts, beyond anything we had ever endured here. Storms, tempests, hurri- canes had been frequent. It seemed as if the great seismic disturbances, which wrought such dire devas- tation last year in the West Indies and other distant places, had caused atmospheric disturbances, electrical storms, all over the world, and not least in these islands. The months of spring and summer had been compara- tively sunless, dull and cold. The fruits of the earth had been poor, both in quality and quantity. The crops had disappointed the farmers, and if it were not for Free Trade (which some were mad enough to attempt to abandon) there would have been want among our people such as this country had not known for years. And this element of disappointment so manifest in this outermost fact of the weather had been present nearly all through the general life. Trade had been hampered. Work had been fitful. The health of the nation, certainly the health of our town, had been unsatisfactory. They had had invasions of disease which had carried off numbers, especially of children,so that there had been sad homes all around them The year had leen disappointing in both national. imperial, and international politics. The year ended in political controversy at home, and in serious rumours of war in the Far East and the near East, all of which, from one reason or other, affected our nation, and caused anxious fears of possible contingencies of strife for which, as a nation, we were absolutely unprepared. Similar disappointment prevailed in most of the churches. It had been a year of almost stagnation in the religious life of the churches—with but few excep- tions. Recent census returns revealed, at any rate, the fact that the Anglican Communion was no longer the Church of the nation, however it might call itself the National Church. And although the Free, Nonconformist Churches might be able to register the larger number of adherents, still, most of them com- plained of spiritual dulness and depression, and the absence of consecration and enterprise for God and for humanity. The greatest missionary societies were hampered by difficulty or by debt,'and the cause of Christian missions was impeded. Sunday schools no longer rallied the young people as once they did, and sanctuary actendance appeared to be a diminishing quan- tity. Personally and domestically also,they might some of them have found the yeandisappointing. It might even have been marked by great and grievous sorrows, tem- poral losses, or devastating bereavements,so that instead of being a red letter, it had been a black letter year. And they might be even thankful that its moments now were but few. They would part with it the more wil- lingly in hope that its successor would bring them better days and fairer experiences. They must not be unduly depressed by disappointing days or years. There were divine and even beneficent meanings in all earthly disappointments. When the human or the earthly proved unsatisfying, in whatever way, it was meant that they should cease setting their affection upon the temporal and transfer it to the eternal and divine. There was only one who could give them inmost and real comfort, whether now as they watched the old year die or when they awaited their own last breath. To Him, therefore let them turn. The congregation spent the last moments of the old year and the first moments of the new year in silent prayer. The pastor then wished all present a Happy New Year. After the singing of a hymn, the congregation departed and there was a general shaking of hands and expressions of goodwill.

OTHER CHURCHES.

There was a crowded congregation at the watchnight service at S. Paul's Church. The Vicar (Rev. W. Bracecamp) delivered an earnest address from the words "Ilhave played the fool and have erred exceed- ingly,' urging the duty of repentance. At St. Barnabas' Church the Rev. P. M. Forrest preached to a good congregation, his text being "What, could ye not watch with me one hour?" His remarks were mainly addressed to those who, in obedience to custom, attended watch-night services and yet were irregular in their attend:tnce at the ordinary services of the Church which were much more important. At Bewser.road Wesleyan Church, the watch-night service wasifairly-well attended. The Rev. R. Wilkins Rees conducted, and in the course of his address spoke on the origin of thewatch-night. He then preceeded to give a brief review of the year and concluded by draw- ing a .suitable application. A collection was taken. on behalf of Dr. Stevenson's homes. _

The service at Bold-street Wesleyan Church was well attended and an appropriate sermon was preached by the Rev, G. H. Carnson, from the words" Let not thy thoughts trouble thee" (I)ltnlalsc. 10v). He gave a graphic sketch of BelShazzar's last feast and referred more particularly to the appeal made to him by Daniel in expounding the words writtenupon the wall. The various lessons of this striking incident were forcibly applied. Golborne-street Baptist Chapel was crowded, the preacher being the Rey. H.. Dunnico. Speaking from the text " And I will bring the blind by a way that they know not." He spoke of the changeableness of life and of the transitory nature of everything around us. No matter how vast a titan's knowledge might be, there were many things that he could not understand, and in regard to which he was in a state of blindness. The message of the text came home to them as showing that although life was a mystery and they could not solve the problem, they had God's promise that he would lead the blind and would not forsake them.

POOR CHILDREN'S TREATS.

Wycliffe 'Hall was filled to its utmost cApacity last evening on the occasion of the annual treat to poor children, provided by the members of the Young Men's Bible Class and friends. Over one thousand poor children from all parts of the town assembled, and ; through the generosity of many friends, they were I supplied with an excellent tea. On entering, each child was the recipient of a paper bag containing current and seed cake and a mince pie. The main hall was found too small for the accommodation of the surprisingly large number of guests from ratter-tatterdom, who assembled, and the adjoining classrooms had to be utilized. A large number of willing assistants served tea into the cups and cans which the children had brought, and in an incredibly short time the whole of the eatables had been removed. A lantern entertainment followed, a collection of suit- able slides being shown °tithe screen byMr. A. Downes and the children shewed their appreciation by hearty cheers. On leaving, each child received an orange and a packet of sweets. Excellent arrangements had been made by a small sub-committee consisting of Mr. Robbins (president of the class), Mr. William Lowe, Mr. Fysh, Mr. J. Garner, Mr. H. Greenleaves, and Mr. R. Lewis. Much of the preliminary work fell on the shoulders of Mr. Joysh, who discharged the onerous duties to the satisfaction of all concerned. I Through the generosity of several local gentlemen, about 300 poor children were entertained at Wycliffe Cockhedge Mission on Friday. After being provided with a substantial tea, a miscellaneous programme was gone through. On leaving the mission each child was presented with a bag containing a roll, a meat pie, an orange, and sweets. A heaTty vote of thanks was given to the donors.

THE TOWN CLERKSHIP OF LEEDS.

Success of a Former Warrington Solicitor.

The Finance Committee of the Leeds Corporation have decided unanimously to recommend the City Council to appoint Mr. R. E. Fox, Town Clerk of Black- burn to be Town Clerk of Leeds in sumession to Mr. W. J. Jeeves, who has resigned to take up practice at the Bar. Mr. Fox is a Lancashire man, a native of the Wigan district, and is 43 years of age, 'though he looks younger. Over the medium height, well-built, clean- shaven, and of legal cast of countenance, Mr. Fox combines in appearance the lawyer and the country gentleman. He is at the head of his profession, for few men have had such an experience in municipal law. Mr. Fox served his articles vath Messrs. Avison and Morton. of Liverpool; was admitted a solicitor in 1883, was for a time with Messrs. Robert Davies and Company, Market Place, Warrington, afterwards at Liverpool, and in 1888 became assistant Town Clerk of Birkenhead. A year later he was appointed Town Clerk of Burnley,. and in 1892 he succeeded Mr. W. E. L. Game as Town Clerk of Blackburn. Nowhere are his abilities more fully recognised than in the town at the head of whose municipal affairs he has stood for eleven years.

===== 0002601_19040102_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
PROMOTION FOR THE WARRINGTON

POSTMASTER.

Mr. Lawrence Elliott Tennent, who has been 'post- master at Warrington for the past eighteen m.onths, has been promoted to the postmastership of Sunder- land, in succession to Mr. WF. West. It is a singular coincidence that Mr. West was also postmaster at Warrington prior to his removal to Sunderland. Mr. West has now retired from the service. It will be remembered that Mr. Tennent succeeded at Warring- ton Mr. J. L. Viney, who came from Douglas, Isle of Man, and is now postmaster of Bolton. Mr. Tennent, who will take up his duties at Sunderland on February Ist, has had a varied experi- ence in Post Office work. He was trained in the general post office at Edinburgh, and his first post- mastership was at Falkirk where he stayed 15 years. He then succeeded to the postmastership of Barrow- in-Furness which he held for eight years, when he was promoted to the Warrington office, .which forms part of the Liverpool district. Although Mr. Tennent has had but a short stay in War- rington, he has introduced additional postal facilities. Two additional town sub-offices have been opened—one at Folly-lane and the other in Lovely- lane. A. new country office' has also been opened at Norcot Brook, near Stretton. The Stockton Heath district has been brought within the limits of the town delivery, as has also a portion of the Latchford dis- trict, as far as Chester-road. The residents in these districts now have four deliveries of letters each day the same as the residents in the Borough. Mr. Tennent has not had the privilege of seeing the new post office erected in Winmarleigh-street —that will be left for his successor. We understand that the plans for the new post office have been prepared, and it is anticipated that early in the new year the contract will be let. •

We understand that Mr. Tenuent will be succeeded at Warrington by Mr. J. Lister, superintendent of the central telegraph office, London,

LIBERALISM AND LABOUR. Mr. Herbert Gladstone asked to secure Unity.

Actuated by a desire to bring about a better under- standing between the progressive forces of Labourism and Liberalism a well known member of the Labour party inthe Accrington Division has addressed the fol- towing communication to Mr. Herbert Gladstone, M.P.— As a twelve years' member of the Labour and Socialist party, anxious to see its success at the polls, and, knowing the interest you take in the Labour cause, as shown in your recent communica- tions to the supporters of Mr. H. M. Hyndman and Mr. Fred Maddison at Burnley, may I ask you pre it is too late to exert your influence in bring- ing about an understanding between the forces of , progress, and so avert division and disaster at the next Parliamentary election, which will undoubtedly be the case if the present course is pursued. The line of demarcation between the three con- tending parties of Liberalism, Labourism. and Socialism is sharply defined, and may be generally summed up as electoral reform, industrial reform, and social reform. It should ho a comparatively easy matter for a small body of intelligent indil viduals, anxious for unity on a "give-and-take " Policy, to outline a working basis by which thiee- cornered contests may be averted. If, then, at this juncture you could succeed in bringing about a conference of the leaders of the three parties you would be striking the best blow against Mr. Chamberlain's fiscal policy, and at the same time giving a healthy fillip to the progressive movement throughout the country. In a few more weeks it may be too late. Can you see your way to act now?

CURIOUS CYCLING FATALITY.

A remarkable fatality happened to a Foleshill cyclist at Corley, near Coventry, on Tuesday. In attempting to mount the machine by the pedal he fell backwards, his head striking the ground. He sustained severe con- ussion, from which he died shortly afterwards.

DRIVING FURIOUSLY AT SIX MILES AN HOUR. Thomas and William Shufflebottom, crockery dealers, of Boundary-road, St. Helens, caused some amusement at the police court on Mouday when they were charged with furiously driving. Sergeant Jackson said that a few minutes past midnight on Saturday, the 19th, the Men were racing down Peasley Cross-lane with two carts They were abreast, and if anything had been coming in the opposite direction there must have beena serious accident. A man named Jones' who was standing up in one of the carts, fell out, but the two carts went dash- on.—Thomas said they were coming back from ‘Vidnes Market at the usual speed. One of the ponies could not gallop at all. The other was trying to pass them, and they were trying to prevent it. (Laughter.) They were driving furiously at the rate of six miles an hour (renewed laughter.) When they got to the stable William asked Thomas, "Where is -your man ?" and Thomas said, "Oh. he's fell out." They went back to look for Jones. If they had not done this they would not have been summoned.—Both defendants were fined 10s.and Is. 6d. costs.

WARRINGTON IN 1903.

A CHAPTER OF LOCAL HISTORY.

Locally, there have been no great outstanding features during the year which has just closed to make it a remarkable one in the history of the Borough. Many events of importance, however, ,have to be chronicled. The two topics which have occu- pied most attention have been the distinctly local one of Municipal Trading, and the great national question raised by Mr. Chamberlain relating to our fiscal policy. When the year opened, the Non-Trading agitation was at its height. At several Council meet- ings there were long and heated debates on the vexed question of an inquiry into the trading , transactions of the Corporation. The first Council meeting in the year was suddenly interrupted by the illness of Coun- cillor Baird, who was seized with a fainting fit. The Council was discussing at the time a resoluti6n by Alderman Monks that a County Court judge or other qualified legal gentleman of equal standing be asked to report in consultation with the Town Clerk on any Charges submitted to him. The Non-Trading members objected to this course and insisted upon an inquiry cOnducted on the lines laid down by Councillor Dodds. At a special meeting,Councillor Tinnion succeeded in carrying a "motion that an investigation should be made and a report presented by two accountants, representing the Non-Trading Association and the Town Council respectively, , The Non-Traders refused to appoint an accountant and a joint committee repre- senting the Council and the Non-Trading Association subsequently drew up a basis of inquiry which proved acceptable to the great majority of the Council and the present inquiry by six Commissioners came into being. At one time there was a serious deadlock on the question as to ' whether a shorthand report of all the proceedings should be taken, but' a settlement has now been arrived at and the investigation proper , has commenced. It is to be hoped that the • ensuing Year will witness, the completion of the Commissioners heavy task and that their findings will at any rate be conclusive. While on the subject of the inquiry, mention may be made of the feelings of the electors, as expressed at the polls The Non-Traders carried all before them in save ra 1 bye - elections, but in the November elections they fared badly and only carried one candidate. During the year, the Council lost the services of two Aldermen who 'perfornied much useful work. Owing to ill-health, Alderman Shaw Green, acting on I the advice of his medical attendant, severed his connection with the Town Council in April. Unfor- tunately, the Alderman did not live long to enjoy his well-earned retirement from public life, for in October he peacefully passed away. As Chair man of the I Finance and Estate Committee he pilote d many im- 1 portant schemes, and his annual financial statements on Budget night were characterised by a clearness I and lucidity which were of great value to the ratepayers. ! In October last, Alderman Monks placed his resigna- tion in the hands of ,the Mayor. In assigning his reason for taking this step, Alderman Monks pointed out that he did not approve of the new-methods which it was thought to introduce into the business of the Council nor the policy of sacrificing "a progressive I efficiency for what was likely to prove a false economy.' His resignation was received with regret. His busi- ness aptitude peculiarly fitted him for the position of , Chairman of the Electricity and Tramway.; Committee and as a keen educationist he threw himself with much earnestness into the work of the New Education i Committee.

The year 1903 has witnessed an important change in the management of educational affairs. The whole of the elementary schools are now under the direction of the newly-formed Education Committee and already many desirable improvements have been effected. The authority has an excellent Chairman in Mr. F. Monks, who devotes much time to the work. The harmony which characterised the early meetings of the com- mittee was rudely disturbed in May last, when a dis- cussion on a proposed increase of salary for the Director of Education, culminated in the resignation of the Chairman. He was prevailed upon to recon- sider his decision and has c mtinued to preside over the deliberations of the committee. Recently he gave an exhaustive review of educational work in .War- rington since the Act came into operation. A step! fraught with great possibilities was taken on September 30th when a seccndary day school was opened in the handsome Technical Institute buildings in Palmyra- square. Accommodation is provided for 128 scholars in addition to 94 pupil teachers, and there is an efficient staff under the superintendence of Mr. Alexander Gow, B.A. Anent the Education question it may be noted that the passive resistance movement spread to Warring- ton. A Citizens' league was:formed under the presi- dency of Mr. Balmford, and the sequel was witnessed in the Borough Police Court in . September, when seven " passive resisters " appeared to answer sum- monses for the non-payment of the Education rate. Prominent among them was the Rev. J. F. Matthews, who has taken up •an uncompromising attitude in reference to the Education Act. Distress warrants were issued and at the close of the sale of goods, a vigorous protest was made against the Act. A letter was read from the Rev. Dr. Clifford, the leader of the Passive Resistance movement, who in endorsing the attitude of the Warrington resisters Said "Rome is on the Rates:" Probably there are few towns where the fiscal question his aroused keener interest than in Warring- ton where so many industries would be effected by any change in tariffs. The !‘ Examiner" threw open' itw columns to manufacturers and others who were willing to ventilate their views on the subject, and the pros and cons have been very fully argued. Mr.- R. Pierpoint, the Member for the Borough has declared himself in favour of Mr. Chamberlain's policy whilst Arr. A. R. Crosfield, the prospective Liberal candidate has been equally emphatic in his loyalty to Free Trade principles. The formation of a local branch of the National Labour Representation Committee, which seeks to obtain the return to Parliament of direct labour representatives, has led to an invitation being extended to Mr. Alexander Wilkie, secretary of the Associated Shipwrights' Society, of Newcastle-on Tyne, to contest the Borough at the next g mend election. • Ile has not yet given his decision. Warrington people will watch with interest the pro- gress of events in the Carnarvon Boroughs constitu- ency, where Mr. IL A. Naylor, who has had lone. associations with Warrington, has entered the field ii opposition to Mr. Lloyd George, M.P. Turning to events immediately local, a matter which caused much 'discussion at the time was the application before the local bench to transfer the licence of the Green Dragon Inn, -Bridge-street, to the Brooklands, Lovely-lane, formerly the residence of Mr. F. Monks. Despite the opposition of the temper- ance workers and residents in the neighbourhood, the transfer was granted, and this growing district has now another licensed house in its midst. The Green Dragon was acquired by the Corporation for the widening of Bridge-street--a work which is being steadily proceeded with. An important development recently has been theTdecision of the Town Council, by a small majority, to • construct a new street from Rylands-street to Sankey-street. There have been several interesting functions in connection with the Corporation during the past year. On June 30th, there was a formal opening of the new waterworks at Delph-lane, which have been con- structed with the view of increasing the supply of ! water for !domestic purposes. The extensions are ! being carried out on a large scale and are not yet complete. The engines and! pumps which have been laid down at the new station are capable, when run- ning at their normal speed, of raising three million gallons of water ! per 24 hours. The ceremony of starting the engines was gracefully performed by Mrs. Francomb, wife of Alderman Francomb (Chairman of the Water Committee). Another interesting ceremony ! was that witnessed in Bank Park on the 2Sth of July ! when Mrs. Crosfield formally opened the Orosfield ! Conservatory, which she kindly presented to the ! town in order to house a choice collection of camellias ! which she had also given to the public on her removal from Walton Lea to Colwyn! Bay. The gift Was accepted on behalf of the inhabitants by the Mayor (Mr. J. C. Parr). In the closing days of his Mayoralty, Mr. J. C. Parr presented a marble bust of the King to the town in conimemoration of the King's Coronation. and Mr. Piirr's Mayoralty. The bust is to be placed on aspedestalin the vestibule of the Town Half.

The inadequate dredging of the Mersey at Warring- ton by the Manchester Ship Canal Company under their agreement with the Corporation has given rise to important litigation during the year. After a long hearing in the Chancery division, Mr. Justice Byrne delivered judgment in April in favour of the plaintiffs (the Corporation and local traders): Mr. Justice Byrne held that the Company had failed to fulfil their obligations, and that the statutory channel of 8 feet depth had never been maintained. Since then, pro- posals have been submitted by the company to construct a river diversion at Walton, with locks and

m:=tl,lkliE

sluices, but the negotiations fell through, and an appeal against Mr. Justice Byrne's ' decision will shortly be heard. The question of compulsory military service has been brought prominently to the fore in Warrington of late. A local branch of the National Service League, which seeks to promote legislation to make it compulsory for young men to undergo a certain period of military training annually, has been formed and Captain G. R. Crosfield is the energetic secretary. As the outcome of an influential meeting held at Appleton Hall there has been considerable discussion and namerous letters for and against the scheme have been contributed to the " Examiner." Warrington has lost many honoured townsmen by death during the past year. On .February 21st Mr. John Houghton, of Sankey, .who was highly esteemed particularly by the employees of Messrs. Houghton at ' Sankey Wire Mills, passed away. Mr. James Muir, for some years a valued member of the Town Council, died on February 23rd and during March the death of Mr. John Cox, Palmyra Square, a well-known local grocers' traveller,' and Mr: T. Smith Burgess, of Walton, had to be recorded. There was sincere regret throughout the town on April ,oth when it became known that Mr. Robert Garnett, J.P., had passed away at his residence, Hall Nook, Penketh. A considerate and popular employer of labour, the deceased gentleman had • gained a warm place in the hearts of many people and at the funeral on Easter Monday impressive scenes were witnessed. On April 15th the death occurred of Mr. W. H. Robinson who in his younger days was, an enthusiastic cricketer and. up to the time of his death discharged with ability the duties of treasurer of the Cricket 'Club. Other well known inhabitants of War- rington who departed this life during May were Mr. Thos. Turner, of Sankey , Bridges, Mr. George Whitehouse and Mr. James Mottram. Councillor John Baird, a .hard-working and much reSpected member of the Council, passed away on June 25th. 'On July 20th a• painful sensation was created by -the death of the Rev. P. R. Pearson, vicar of St. ljairiess, Latchford, and On August 20th the congregation at Emmanuel Church had to lament the- loss of their vicar, the Rev. A. Cr. D. Gee. Mr. Thomas Fletcher, the pioneer of the gas stove industry and head of the firm of Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Company Ltd., passed •away at his residence at , G-rappenhall on September 20th. To the death of Mr. J. Shaw Green, J.P., which occurred on October 20th, we have already alluded. On November 10th, the Town Council sustained another loss by the demise o Councillor John Brophy. Mr. Joseph Knowles, a well-known agriculturalist, died at his residence at Appleton on November 15th, and on December Bth the sad end of Mr. George A.Morkillananaging chemist at the Warrington Chemical Company's work at Sankey Bridges, created widespread regret. There have been several changes in local ecclesiasti— cal circles during the twelve months embraced in this short review. The Rev. F. A. Screeton resigned the living of Christ Church, Latchford, in August, to take charge of the work ht St. Thomas's, Hyde, and he will shortly be succeeded by the Rev. G. C. Briggs, of St. Barnabas' Mission,Church Chester.TO-day the Rev. H. B. Firth, senior carafe at Runcorn Parish Church, will be inducted to the living of St. James', Latch— ford, rendered vac.un by. the lamentable, death of the Rev. F. R. Pearson. No appointment has yet been made of a minister to succeed the late Rev. A. G-. D. Gee at Emmanuel Church, 80ld-street. In February the Rev. E. W. Lummis resigned the pastorate of Cairo-street Unitarian Chapel and the Rev. C. Harvey Cook, of Holbeck, Leeds, has now .entered' upon the duties.

Several disastrous fires in the town and neighbour- hood have to be noted. On April 19th the venerable Parish Church of Newchurch was the scene of ae out- break which resulted in' the complete destruction Of the historic edifice. • One of the most serious fires ever witnessed in Warrington .took place on June, Bth when the large tanneiy. of Messrs.. Fleming and Company was gutted. Another ,conflagration which did great damage occurred on August 21st at Messrs. Greenhalgh and Company's works. „Without a reference to the. Climatic conditions our review would be incomplete. On the whole we may complain of having been rather badly treated by the Clerk of the Weather. Warrin,tonians will not readily forget the abnormal rainfall ;during the months which are usually devoted to holiday-making and the members of the Police Force had special cause to feel aggrieved in having to postpone their anntml sports.- Even then the weather was of a, wretched description and consequently the meeting was not as successful as had been acticipated. A gale of terrific violence swept over the town on February 27th and the fall of a hoarding resulted in the death of an ironworker who was proceeding over Belvsey Bridge to liie.forge.

In conclusion, let us express the hope that the year which has just been ushered in will be one of general prosperity for the "good old town" and its inhabit- ants. Many branches of trade appear to be far from brisk at present, and we sincerely trust that brirghte times are in store.

COUNT 17 BOROUGH OF WARRING VON. THE EMPLOYMENT OF CHILDREN Acr, 1933. STREET TRADING. TAKE NOTICE. That on and after the Ist JANUARY. 1904, NO CHILD under the ace of Eleven Years SHALL BE EMPLOYHD in STREET TRADING. " S MEET TRADING." includes the hawking of newspapers, matches, flowers, and other articles, playing. singing, or performing for profit. shoe blacking. and an other like occupation carried on in streets or public places. RESTRICTION ON EMPOYMENT OF CHILDREN UNDER 14. NO CHILD under 14 years of age SHALL BE EMPLOYED before 6 o'clo"k in the morning or after 9 o'clock at night. " EMPLOYMENT " includes employment in any labour exercised by way of trade or for the purposes of gain, whether the gain he to the child or t s any other person, and includes street trading as above defined. PENALTIES. For the first offence 405.. and for a second or subsequent offenoe £5. If any parent or guardian of a. child employs or conduces to the commission of an offence by wilful default or by habitually neglecting to exercise due care, he will be liable on summary conviction to the like fines.
